Light propagation in 2PN approximation in the monopole and quadrupole field of a body at rest: Initial value problem
Sven Zschocke

Abstract
The light trajectory in the gravitational field of one body at rest with monopole and quadrupole structure is determined in the second post-Newtonian (2PN) approximation. The terms in the geodesic equation for light rays are separated into time-independent tensorial coefficients and four kind of time-dependent scalar functions. Accordingly, the first and second integration of geodesic equation can be reduced in each case to only four kind of scalar master integrals. These integrals can be solved in closed form by recurrence relations. The 2PN terms of monopole and quadrupole contribute less than 1 1 nano-arcsecond to the total light deflection. There are, however, enhanced terms in the 2PN light deflection, both in case of monopole and quadrupole. These enhanced 2PN terms are caused by the use of an impact vector which is indispensable for modeling of real astrometric measurements. In case of grazing light rays at Jupiter and Saturn, the enhanced 2PN terms, caused by the quadrupole structure of the body, amount up to 0.95 0.95 micro-arcseconds and 0.29 0.29 micro-arcseconds, respectively. Thus, the 2PN quadrupole terms are relevant for high-precision astrometry on the sub-micro-arcsecond scale of accuracy.

95.10.Jk, 95.10.Ce, 95.30.Sf, 04.25.Nx, 04.80.Cc

Introduction

Space-based astrometry missions of the European Space Agency (ESA) have initiated a renewed advance of highly precise astrometric measurements.
The Hipparcos mission (nominal mission: 1989-1993)
Hipparcos
;
Hipparcos1
;
Hipparcos2
has reached the milli-arcsecond (mas)
level in astrometric precision, while the Gaia mission (nominal mission: 2013-2018)
GAIA
;
GAIA_ESA
;
GAIA1

has reached the micro-arcsecond (
\mu
as
) scale of accuracy in angular observations
GAIA_DR1_1
;
GAIA_DR2_1
;
Gaia_Archive
.

While micro-arcsecond astrometry has been realized both theoretically and technologically within the Gaia mission, the dawning of
sub-micro-arcsecond (sub-
\mu
as
) or even nano-arcsecond (nas) astrometry has come in the strategic focus of
astronomers
Proceeding_Sub_Microarcsecond_Astrometry
;
article_sub_micro_4
. In particular,
ESA has recently called for new science themes to be investigated in terms of feasibility and technological developments, some of which may become candidates
for possible astrometry missions within the ESA Science Program in future
ESA
. For instance, among several others,
the M-5 mission Gaia-NIR
Gaia_NIR
has been proposed to ESA to operate in the near infrared spectrum and in the
\mu
as
and sub-
\mu
as
domain in
astrometric accuracy. Recently, in the final recommendations of the ESA Senior Science Committee
Voyage_2050

the Gaia-NIR mission has been selected as one possible candidate for M-sized astrometry mission.
Another astrometry mission is the proposed M-5 mission Theia
Theia

which is operating in the domain of sub-
\mu
as
in astrometric precision.
A further candidate is the space-based mission NEAT (Nearby Earth Astrometric Telescope)
NEAT1
,
originally designed for an astrometric precision of about
50
nas.
The proposed NEAT mission consists in adopting a long focal length realized by a formation flight configuration
of two satellites.
Also feasibility studies of earth-bounded telescopes are under consideration aiming at
accuracies up to about 10 nas
nas_telescopes
.

The impressive progress of astrometric accuracies necessitates the use of general relativity to model the reduction of
observational data. Such relativistic modeling of observational data consists of several aspects and issues which need to be solved

Proceeding_Sub_Microarcsecond_Astrometry
:
a set of several astronomical reference systems, relativistic description of the process of observations, relativistic equations of motions
of Solar System bodies, etc. A scheme of general relativistic modeling of observational data is given by Fig.
1
in

Klioner2003b
. One important aspect thereof concerns the relativistic modeling of propagation of a light signal from the celestial
light source (e.g. star, quasar, exoplanet) through the gravitational field of the Solar System towards the observer. The present-day
relativistic models, like the Gaia Relativistic Model (GREM)
Brumberg1991
;
Klioner2003a
;
KlionerKopeikin1992

and the Relativistic Astrometric Model (RAMOD)
RAMOD1
;
RAMOD2
;
RAMOD3
;
RAMOD4
;
RAMOD5
as used for data reduction
of ESA astrometry mission Gaia, are designed for relativistic astrometry at
\mu
as
-level of accuracy.
These models need considerably to be refined for modeling observational data on the sub-
\mu
as
-level of accuracy.
In particular, unprecedented accuracies on the sub-
\mu
as
-level of accuracy necessitate considerable advancements in the theory of light propagation
in the Solar System.

For determining the light trajectory in curved space-time
one needs to solve the geodesic equation
Einstein2
;
MTW
;
Brumberg1991

(e.g. Eqs. (1.2.46) - (1.2.47) in
Brumberg1991
))

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}x^{\alpha}\left(\lambda\right)}{d\lambda^{2}}+\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\,\frac{dx^{\mu}\left(\lambda\right)}{d\lambda}\,\frac{dx^{\nu}\left(\lambda\right)}{d\lambda}=0\,,

(1)

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}\,\frac{dx^{\alpha}\left(\lambda\right)}{d\lambda}\,\frac{dx^{\beta}\left(\lambda\right)}{d\lambda}=0\;,

(2)

where
x^{\alpha}\left(\lambda\right)
are the four-coordinates of the light signal which depend on some affine curve-parameter
\lambda
.
The solutions of geodesic equation (
1
) for light rays must satisfy the null-condition (
2
) as additional constraint
along the light trajectory.
The Christoffel symbols in (
1
) are given by

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\,g^{\alpha\beta}\left(\frac{\partial g_{\beta\mu}}{\partial x^{\nu}}+\frac{\partial g_{\beta\nu}}{\partial x^{\mu}}-\frac{\partial g_{\mu\nu}}{\partial x^{\beta}}\right),

(3)

so they are functions of the metric tensor,
where
g^{\alpha\beta}
and
g_{\alpha\beta}
are the contravariant and covariant components of the metric tensor of space-time; the metric signature
is assumed to be
\left(-,+,+,+\right)
.

The metric tensor of some Solar System body, which enters the geodesic equation (
1
) via the Christoffel symbols,
cannot be given in it’s exact form and one has to resort rather than to approximation schemes.
Such an approximation scheme is provided by the post-Newtonian expansion of the metric tensor, which
is an expansion in terms of inverse powers of the speed of light, given by:

g_{\alpha\beta}=\eta_{\alpha\beta}+h^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}+h^{(3)}_{\alpha\beta}+h^{(4)}_{\alpha\beta}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-5}\right),

(4)

where
h^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}={\cal O}\left(c^{-2}\right)
and
h^{(3)}_{\alpha\beta}={\cal O}\left(c^{-3}\right)
are the 1PN and 1.5PN
corrections and
h^{(4)}_{\alpha\beta}={\cal O}\left(c^{-4}\right)
is the 2PN correction of the light-ray metric;
see also endnote
1
1

1

In celestial mechanics another notation is used (in celestial mechanics the term
post-Newtonian correction
includes

h^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}
and
h^{(3)}_{\alpha\beta}
and
h^{(4)}_{00}
) and, therefore,
our notation for the terms in the expansion (
4
) is sometimes called
light-ray metric
. In our investigation
we will call (
4
) just metric and will drop the term
light-ray metric
.
.
The justification of such an expansion is based on the fact that the gravitational
fields in the Solar System are weak and the velocities of matter are slow,

\frac{m}{P}\ll 1\quad{\rm and}\quad\frac{v}{c}\ll 1\;,

(5)

where
m=GM/c^{2}
is the Schwarzschild radius of some Solar System body with mass
M
and radius
P
, and
v
stands representatively for
possible velocities of matter (orbital velocities, rotational motions, inner circulations, oscillations, etc.).

Let the curved space-time assumed to be covered by the four-coordinates
x^{\mu}=\left(ct,\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)
, where
x
are the spatial coordinates
and
t
is the coordinate time.
A unique interpretation of astrometric observations requires the determination of light trajectory
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t\right)
as function of coordinate time.
Consider a light signal which is emitted by some light source having four-coordinates
\left(ct_{0},\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\right)
. In the Newtonian theory that signal would
travel along some straight line:
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\left(t\right)=\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}+c\left(t-t_{0}\right)\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}
in some unit-direction
\sigma
.
In curved space-time the light trajectory is given by a complicated curvilinear trajectory

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t\right)=\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}+c\left(t-t_{0}\right)\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t,t_{0}\right),

(6)

where
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t,t_{0}\right)
are corrections to the unperturbed light ray.
More precisely, these statements are valid in case of a weak gravitational field and ordinary topology of space-time.
Inserting the post-Newtonian expansion of the metric tensor (
4
) into the
geodesic equation (
1
) implies a corresponding post-Newtonian expansion of the light trajectory,

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}+c\left(t-t_{0}\right)\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1.5PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+{\cal O}\left(c^{-5}\right),

(7)

where the post-Newtonian corrections
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}={\cal O}\left(c^{-2}\right)
and
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1.5PN}={\cal O}\left(c^{-3}\right)

are the 1PN and 1.5PN terms, while the post-post-Newtonian correction
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 2PN}={\cal O}\left(c^{-4}\right)
is the
2PN term of the light ray.

Remarkable advancement in determining the 1PN and 1.5PN terms
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}
and
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1.5PN}
has been achieved during recent decades.
These advancements were also triggered by the ESA astrometry missions Hipparcos and Gaia. The problem of light propagation in
1PN and 1.5PN approximation has been solved for the following systems: monopoles at rest
Brumberg1991
,
monopoles in motion
Klioner1991
;
Klioner2003b
;
KS1999
, spin-dipoles at rest
Klioner1991
, spin-dipoles in motion
KopeikinMashhoon2002
,
quadrupoles at rest
Klioner1991
, quadrupoles in motion
KopeikinMakarov2007
, full set of mass-multipoles and spin multipoles at rest

Kopeikin1997
;
KopeikinKorobkov2005
;
KopeikinKorobkovPolnarev2006
, full set of mass-multipoles and spin multipoles in slow motion
Zschocke1
;
Zschocke2

(the calculations in
KS1999
;
KopeikinMashhoon2002
;
KopeikinMakarov2007
are performed in the first order of the post-Minkowskian scheme, that means
they are valid for arbitrary motions, especially also for ultra-relativistic motions of the bodies).

However, much less is known about light propagation in 2PN approximation, that means for the terms
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 2PN}
in (
7
).
In fact, the light trajectory in 2PN approximation has only been determined in the gravitational field
of spherically symmetric bodies, namely either monopoles at rest or monopoles in slow motion:

\bullet

2PN light trajectory in the field of one monopole at rest
Brumberg1991
;
Brumberg1987
;
KlionerKopeikin1992
;
Teyssandier
;
AshbyBertotti2010
;
Deng_2015
;
Xie_Huang
;
Minazzoli2
,

\bullet

2PN light trajectory in the field of two point-like bodies of a binary system
Bruegmann2005
,

\bullet

2PN light trajectory in field of one arbitrarily moving monopole
Zschocke3
;
Zschocke4
;
Zschocke5
.

These results are by far not sufficient for astrometry on the sub-
\mu
as
-level of accuracy
Proceeding_Sub_Microarcsecond_Astrometry
.
Especially, it is a well-known fact that astrometric accuracies on sub-micro-arcsecond or nano-arcsecond level cannot be achieved
without accounting further second post-Newtonian terms in the theory of light propagation

Xu_Wu
;
Xu_Gong_Wu_Soffel_Klioner
;
MC2009
;
Deng_Xie
;
2PN_Light_PropagationA
;
Deng_2015
;
Xie_Huang
;
Minazzoli2
;
XWKS
;
KS
;
Conference_Cambridge
;
Talk_Klioner
.
In particular, real Solar System bodies are not simply monopoles but can be of arbitrary shape. To account for the arbitrary shape of the bodies
one utilizes a decomposition of the gravitational field in terms of multipoles, which are certain integrals over the stress-energy tensor of these
bodies
MTW
;
Poisson_Will
;
Kopeikin_Efroimsky_Kaplan
;
Poisson_Lecture_Notes
.
The first term in such an expansion is the monopole term. After the dipole term, which vanishes if the origin of the spatial coordinates is
located at the center of mass of the body, the next term in the multipole-decomposition
is the quadrupole term. Accordingly, in this investigation the geodesic equation for light rays
in the quadrupole field of one massive body at rest is considered in 2PN approximation and the
impact of the quadrupole structure on light deflection is determined.

The manuscript is organized as follows:
The metric of one Solar System body being of arbitrary shape and inner structure is presented in Section
II
and the exact geodesic equation
is given in Section
III
. The unperturbed light ray is described in Section
IV
. In Section
V
and
VI
the light
propagation in the gravitational field of one massive body at rest with monopole and quadrupole structure is determined in 1PN and 2PN approximation. The total
light deflection is calculated in Section
VII
. The problem of
enhanced terms
is considered in Section
VIII
. Finally,
a summary and outlook can be found in Section
IX
. The notation and some details of the calculations are relegated to a set of several appendices.

II
Metric of a Solar System body

In this investigation we will consider the propagation of a light signal in the gravitational field generated by one Solar System body at rest.
The metric tensor enters the geodesic equation (
1
) via the Christoffel symbols (
3
).
Hence, the first step is to consider the metric of a Solar System body.
If the gravitational fields are weak, then the so-called post-Minkowskian (PM) expansion (weak-field expansion)
of the metric tensor can be utilized, which is an expansion of the metric tensor in powers of the gravitational constant

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}\left(t,\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta_{\alpha\beta}+\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}G^{n}h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left({\rm nPM}\right)}\left(t,\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right),

(8)

where
\eta_{\alpha\beta}={\rm diag}\left(-1,+1,+1,+1\right)
is the flat Minkowskian space-time and
h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left({\rm nPM}\right)}

are small metric perturbations, which vanish at Minkowskian past infinity
{\cal J}_{M}^{-}
(cf. Fig.
34.2
of Section
34
in
MTW
)

\displaystyle\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty\atop t+\frac{x}{c}={\rm const}}g_{\alpha\beta}\left(t,\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)=\eta_{\alpha\beta}\;.

(9)

In reality the massive Solar System bodies are of arbitrary shape and inner structure and the bodies can be in arbitrarily rotational and oscillating motions.
The Multipolar Post-Minkowskian (MPM) approach provides a robust framework to solve Einstein’s field equations for such complex gravitational systems.
The MPM formalism has originally been introduced in
Thorne
and later considerably been extended in a series
of articles
Blanchet_Damour1
;
Blanchet_Damour2
;
Blanchet_Damour3
;
Blanchet_Damour4
;
2PN_Metric1
;
Multipole_Damour_2

to determine the metric density,
\overline{g}^{\alpha\beta}=\sqrt{-g}\,g^{\alpha\beta}
,
in the exterior of an isolated source of matter, where
g={\rm det}\left(g_{\mu\nu}\right)
is the determinant of the metric tensor. In the MPM approach
the space-time is covered by harmonic coordinates, in line with the IAU (International Astronomical Union) resolutions
IAU_Resolution1
;
harmonic coordinates are Cartesian-like coordinates which are Cartesian at spatial infinity and they are defined by the
gauge-condition
\overline{g}^{\alpha\beta}_{\;\;\;\,,\,\beta}=0

MTW
;
Poisson_Will
;
Kopeikin_Efroimsky_Kaplan
;
Poisson_Lecture_Notes
.
An isolated gravitational system is defined by the Fock-Sommerfeld boundary conditions, which imply flatness of the metric at spatial infinity
(
9
) and the constraint of no-incoming gravitational radiation is imposed at Minkowskian past null infinity

Fock
;
KlionerKopeikin1992
;
IAU_Resolution1
;
Kopeikin_Efroimsky_Kaplan
;
Radiation_Condition
(e.g. Eq. (10) in
Radiation_Condition
).

The MPM approach was mainly developed for theoretical understanding of the generation of gravitational waves by some isolated source of matter,
like inspiralling binary stars which consist of compact objects like black holes or neutron stars. The compact source of matter can of course also
be interpreted as some massive Solar System body. Thus, the MPM approach allows us to obtain the metric perturbations in (
8
).

The MPM formalism utilizes the post-Minkowskian expansion of metric density. The most general solution for the metric density is given
in terms of six so-called source-multipoles
Blanchet4
;
Blanchet6
. Harmonic coordinates are not unique but allow for a residual gauge transformation.
Using this freedom in the choice of harmonic coordinates, it has been demonstrated in
Thorne
;
Blanchet_Damour1
that the metric density can
finally be expressed in terms of only two symmetric trace-free (STF) multipoles: mass-multipoles
\hat{M}_{L}
(describing shape, inner structure and
oscillations of the body) and spin-multipoles
\hat{S}_{L}
(describing rotational motions and inner currents of the body). For an explicit form of
these multipoles in the first post-Minkowskian approximation it is referred to Eqs. (5.33) and (5.35) in
Multipole_Damour_2
.

The metric density
\overline{g}^{\alpha\beta}
and the metric tensor
g_{\alpha\beta}
contain the same physical information, because
they are related bijective to each other
MTW
;
Poisson_Will
;
Kopeikin_Efroimsky_Kaplan
;
Poisson_Lecture_Notes
; some relations
are also given in Appendix D in
Zschocke_2PM_Metric
.
From the metric density
\overline{g}^{\alpha\beta}
one may deduce the metric tensor
g_{\alpha\beta}
. A detailed description
about how to obtain the metric tensor
g_{\alpha\beta}
in (
8
) from the metric density

\overline{g}^{\alpha\beta}
of the MPM formalism can be found in our recent article
Zschocke_2PM_Metric
.
In this way one arrives at the following post-Minkowskian expansion of the metric tensor for a Solar System body in the so-called canonical harmonic gauge

Thorne
;
Blanchet_Damour1
;
Book_PN
;
Poujade_Blanchet
;
Blanchet_Damour3
;
Zschocke_2PM_Metric
;
Proceeding_Zschocke

(cf. Eqs. (78) and (79) in
Zschocke_2PM_Metric
)

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}\left(t,\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta_{\alpha\beta}+\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}G^{n}h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left({\rm nPM}\right)}\!\left(\hat{M}_{L}\left(s\right),\hat{S}_{L}\left(s\right)\right).

(10)

In (
10
) the origin of the spatial axes of the coordinate system can be assumed to be located at the center
of mass of the body and
s=t-x/c
is the retarded time (travel time of a light signal from the field point
\left(t,\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)
to the center of the
coordinate system). For the concrete form of the 1PM term
h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left({\rm 1PM}\right)}
and 2PM term

h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left({\rm 2PM}\right)}
we refer to Eqs. (109) - (111) and Eqs. (115) - (117), respectively, in
Zschocke_2PM_Metric
.

If the speed of the matter (e.g. oscillations, rotational motions and inner currents of the body) is small compared to the speed of light,
then one may utilize the post-Newtonian (PN) expansion (weak-field slow-motion expansion), which is a expansion of the metric tensor in inverse powers of the
speed of light, and reads up to terms of the order
{\cal O}\left(c^{-5}\right)
as follows,

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}\left(t,\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta_{\alpha\beta}+h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(2\right)}\left(t,\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)+h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(3\right)}\left(t,\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)+h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(4\right)}\left(t,\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)+{\cal O}\left(c^{-5}\right),

(11)

where
h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(n\right)}={\cal O}\left(c^{-n}\right)
are small perturbations to the flat Minkowskian space-time metric. The expansion
(
11
) follows from (
8
) in case of slow motions.
The post-Newtonian expansion (
11
) is actually a non-analytic series, because at higher orders
n\geq 8

non-analytic terms involving powers of logarithms occur
Thorne
;
Blanchet_Damour1
;
Book_PN
;
Poujade_Blanchet
;
Blanchet_Damour3
.
From the MPM formalism it follows that the most general PN solution is given in terms of only two mass-multipoles and spin-multipoles

Zschocke_2PM_Metric

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}\left(t,\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)=\eta_{\alpha\beta}+h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(2\right)}\left(\hat{M}_{L}\left(s\right)\right)+h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(3\right)}\left(\hat{M}_{L}\left(s\right),\hat{S}_{L}\left(s\right)\right)+h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(4\right)}\left(\hat{M}_{L}\left(s\right),\hat{S}_{L}\left(s\right)\right)+{\cal O}\left(c^{-5}\right),

(12)

where the metric perturbations
h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(2\right)}
and
h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(3\right)}
can be read off from Eqs. (109) - (111)
in
Zschocke_2PM_Metric
, while
h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(4\right)}
is given by Eqs. (115) - (117) in
Zschocke_2PM_Metric
.

In case of stationary source of matter the multipoles and the metric perturbations are time-independent and then the post-Newtonian expansion
of the metric tensor reads

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta_{\alpha\beta}+h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(2\right)}\left(\hat{M}_{L}\right)+h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(3\right)}\left(\hat{S}_{L}\right)+h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(4\right)}\left(\hat{M}_{L}\right)+{\cal O}\left(c^{-5}\right).

(13)

The terms
h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(2\right)}
are given by Eqs. (127) and (129) in
Zschocke_2PM_Metric
,
the term
h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(3\right)}
is given by Eqs. (128) in
Zschocke_2PM_Metric
,
and
h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(4\right)}
is given by Eqs. (134) - (136) in
Zschocke_2PM_Metric
.
The STF multipoles are given by Eqs. (125) and (126) in
Zschocke_2PM_Metric
and read

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{3}x\;\hat{x}_{L}\;\frac{T^{00}+T^{kk}}{c^{2}}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-5}\right),

(14)

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm STF}_{L}\int d^{3}x\;\hat{x}_{L-1}\;\epsilon_{i_{l}jk}\;x^{j}\;\frac{T^{0k}}{c}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-5}\right),

(15)

where
T^{\alpha\beta}
is the stress-energy tensor of the body and the integration runs over the three-dimensional volume of the body;
the STF notation is given in Appendix
B
.

As described in the introductory Section, our knowledge about 2PN effects in the theory of light propagation in the Solar System is restricted
to the case of light propagation in the gravitational field generated by bodies with mass-monopole structure.
In this investigation we will go beyond the mass-monopole
approximation and consider the problem of light propagation in the 2PN approximation in the field of one body at rest with time-independent monopole
and quadrupole structure, while higher mass-multipoles are neglected. Furthermore, we do not consider any rotational motions or inner circulations
of the massive body. Accordingly, there are no spin-multipoles and, therefore, no 1.5PN terms in the metric tensor.
The mass-dipole term vanishes
M_{a}=0
because the origin of the coordinate system is assumed to be
at the center of mass of the body. That means for our purposes the metric (
13
) simplifies to the form

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta_{\alpha\beta}+h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(2\right)}\left(M,\hat{M}_{ab}\right)+h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(4\right)}\left(M,\hat{M}_{ab}\right)+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right).

(16)

There are no terms of the order
{\cal O}\left(c^{-3}\right)
and
{\cal O}\left(c^{-5}\right)
in the metric tensor
(
16
) because mass-multipoles are assumed to be time-independent and spin-multipoles are neglected.
The metric perturbations in (
16
) have been obtained in our article
Zschocke_2PM_Metric
and they are given below:
the 1PN terms
h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(2\right)}
are given by Eqs. (
41
) and (
42
)
and the 2PN terms
h_{\alpha\beta}^{\left(4\right)}
are given by Eqs. (
69
) and (
70
).
The monopole and the STF quadrupole moment in (
16
) are given by

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{3}x\,\frac{T^{00}+T^{kk}}{c^{2}}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-5}\right),

(17)

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{3}x\,\hat{x}_{ab}\,\frac{T^{00}+T^{kk}}{c^{2}}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-5}\right),

(18)

where
\hat{x}_{ab}=\hat{x}^{ab}=x^{a}x^{b}-\delta^{ab}\,x^{2}/3
.
The integrals in (
17
) and (
18
)
run over the three-dimensional volume of the body, where the stress-energy tensor of the body is assumed to be time-independent;
see also endnote
2
2

2

For matter we adopt the post-Newtonian statements
T^{\alpha\beta}={\cal O}\left(c^{2},c^{1},c^{0}\right)

DSX1
. The given order in Eqs. (
17
) - (
18
) follows from Eqs. (6.2) - (6.3) in
Blanchet4
;
cf. Eqs. (5.11a) and (5.11b) in
Gauge_Transformation
or Eqs. (99a) - (99b) in
Blanchet6
.
.
The explicit mathematical form of the quadrupole moment (
18
) is given below by Eq. (
105
) for the model
of an axial symmetric body, which is a good approximation for the giant planets of the Solar System;
see also endnote
3
3

3

In the literature the mass-quadrupole moment is sometimes introduced in the following form (e.g. Eq. (3)
in
Klioner1991
)

M_{ab}=\int d^{3}x\,x^{ab}\,\left(T^{00}+T^{kk}\right)/c^{2}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-5}\right)

where
x^{ab}=x^{a}x^{b}
so that
M_{ab}
is not traceless:
M_{kk}=\delta^{ab}\,M_{ab}\neq 0
.
However, the trace of the quadrupole moment (or any higher moments) does no contribute to the metric tensor
due to STF relation (
43
).
In general, trace terms of mass-moments or spin-moments cancel each other in the metric tensor and, therefore, the calculations are considerably
simplified if the metric tensor is expressed in terms of STF multipoles.
.

III
The exact geodesic equation

The trajectory of a light signal propagating in curved space-time is determined by the geodesic equation (
1
) and
isotropic condition (
2
), which in terms of coordinate time read as follows
Kopeikin_Efroimsky_Kaplan
;
Brumberg1991
;
MTW

(e.g. Eqs. (1.2.48) - (1.2.49) in
Brumberg1991
or Eqs. (7.20) - (7.23) in
Kopeikin_Efroimsky_Kaplan
):

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}^{i}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}+\Gamma^{i}_{\mu\nu}\frac{\dot{x}^{\mu}\left(t\right)}{c}\frac{\dot{x}^{\nu}\left(t\right)}{c}-\Gamma^{0}_{\mu\nu}\frac{\dot{x}^{\mu}\left(t\right)}{c}\frac{\dot{x}^{\nu}\left(t\right)}{c}\frac{\dot{x}^{i}\left(t\right)}{c}=0\;,

(19)

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}\,\frac{\dot{x}^{\alpha}\left(t\right)}{c}\,\frac{\dot{x}^{\beta}\left(t\right)}{c}=0\;,

(20)

where
g_{\alpha\beta}
are the covariant components of the metric tensor of space-time and a dot denotes total derivative
with respect to coordinate time. The isotropic condition (
20
) states that light trajectories are null rays.
This condition is satisfied at any point along the light trajectory; cf. text below Eq. (
63
)
as well as text below Eqs. (
85
) and (
102
).
The Christoffel symbols in (
19
) are given by Eq. (
3
).

The metric and the Christoffel symbols (
3
) are functions of the entire space-time
(t,\mbox{\boldmath$x$})
, while if one inserts
these symbols in the geodesic equation (
19
) then the metric and the Christoffel symbols become relevant only
at the coordinates of the photon
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t\right)
. Consequently, the derivatives of the metric tensor
contained in the Christoffel symbols (
3
) of the geodesic equation (
19
) must be taken along the light trajectory,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g_{\alpha\beta}}{\partial x^{\mu}}=\frac{\partial g_{\alpha\beta}\left(t,\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)}{\partial x^{\mu}}\Bigg|_{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}=\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\mbox{$\left(t\right)$}}\;,

(21)

where
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t\right)
is the exact light trajectory. The equation (
21
) means that
the differentiations of the metric tensor in (
3
) have to be performed with respect to the space-time coordinates,
and afterwards the light trajectory has to be substituted.

The geodesic equation (
19
) is a differential equation of second order of one variable,
t
,
thus a well-defined initial-value problem
(Cauchy problem) implies two initial conditions: the initial unit direction
\mu
of the light signal and the spatial position of the light source

\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}
at the moment of emission of the light signal:

\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t\right)}{\left|\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t\right)\right|}\bigg|_{t=t_{0}}\quad{\rm with}\quad\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}=1\;,

(22)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\;

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t\right)\,\,\bigg|_{t=t_{0}}\;.

(23)

A graphical elucidation of these vectors is given in Figure
1
.

III.1
The first integration of the exact geodesic equation

As stated above, a unique solution of the first integration of geodesic equation (
19
) requires the initial condition
(
22
) for the direction
\mu
of the light ray which is emitted by the light source. The initial condition (
22
) can be replaced
by the initial condition of the direction
\sigma
of the light ray at past infinity

Brumberg1991
;
Kopeikin_Efroimsky_Kaplan
;
KlionerKopeikin1992
;
Kopeikin1997
;
KSGE
;
Zschocke1
;
Zschocke2
;
Zschocke3
;
Zschocke4
;
Zschocke5
,

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}=\frac{\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t\right)}{c}\bigg|_{t=-\infty}\quad{\rm with}\quad\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}=1\;,

(24)

with
\sigma
being the unit-direction of the light ray at past infinity.
For a graphical elucidation of these statements see also Figure
1
.
There is a one-to-one correspondence between condition (
24
) and (
22
). In fact,
one may find a unique relation between the tangent vectors
\sigma
and
\mu
(cf. Section
3.2.3
in
Brumberg1991
).
The advantage of (
24
) is that the integration procedure and the expressions become simpler when using (
24
)
instead of (
22
).
Then, the first integration of geodesic equation (
19
) yields the coordinate velocity of the light signal,

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t\right)}{c}=\int\limits_{-\infty}^{t}dc{\rm t}\,\frac{\ddot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left({\rm t}\right)}{c^{2}}=\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}+\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t\right)}{c}\;,

(25)

where the integration variable
{\rm t}
(roman style) runs from lower limit of integration
-\infty
to the upper limit of integration
t
(mathnormal style)
and where
\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t\right)/c
denotes the correction to the unit-direction
\sigma
of the light ray at past infinity, so that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow-\infty}\;\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t\right)}{c}=0\;.

(26)

Let us notice that (
24
) in combination with (
9
) implies that the null condition (
20
) is satisfied
at past infinity.

III.2
The second integration of the exact geodesic equation

As stated above, a unique solution of the second integration of geodesic equation (
19
) requires the initial condition (
23
)

Brumberg1991
;
Kopeikin_Efroimsky_Kaplan
;
KlionerKopeikin1992
;
Kopeikin1997
;
KSGE
;
Zschocke1
;
Zschocke2
;
Zschocke3
;
Zschocke4
;
Zschocke5
,

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}=\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t\right)\bigg|_{t=t_{0}}\;,

(27)

with
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}
being the initial position of the light source at the moment of the emission of the light signal.
The second integration of geodesic equation (
19
) yields the trajectory of the light signal,

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t\right)=\int\limits_{t_{0}}^{t}dc{\rm t}\,\frac{\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left({\rm t}\right)}{c}=\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}+c\left(t-t_{0}\right)\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t,t_{0}\right),

(28)

where the integration variable
{\rm t}
(roman style) runs from lower limit of integration
t_{0}
to the upper limit of
integration
t
(mathnormal style) and where
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t,t_{0}\right)
denotes the corrections to the unperturbed light trajectory, so that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{0}}\;\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t,t_{0}\right)=0\;.

(29)

IV
The unperturbed light ray

The geodesic equation for a light signal, Eqs. (
19
) and (
20
), will be solved by iteration.
In the zeroth approximation, that is the Newtonian limit, the light signal propagates along a straight line.
We assume the light signal to be emitted at the position of the light source,
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\,
,
and travels along the unit-vector
\sigma
. Thus, the coordinate velocity of this
so-called unperturbed light ray reads

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm N}}{c}=\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\;,

(30)

where the subindex
{\rm N}
stands for Newtonian.
Then, the spatial position of the unperturbed light ray at coordinate time
t
is given by

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}+c\left(t-t_{0}\right)\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\;.

(31)

Furthermore, we notice the absolute value of the spatial coordinate of the unperturbed light ray,

\displaystyle x_{\rm N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{x_{0}^{2}+2\,c\,\left(t-t_{0}\right)\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\right)+c^{2}\left(t-t_{0}\right)^{2}}\;.

(32)

The iterative approach is based on the unperturbed light ray, that means the subsequent calculations are
finally expressed in terms of (
30
) and (
31
).
A further important quantity is the impact vector
\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}
and its absolute value
d_{\sigma}
of the
unperturbed light ray, which are defined as follows (cf. Eq. (55) in
Article_Zschocke1
)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\right)=\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\right)\quad{\rm hence}\quad\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}=0\;,

(33)

\displaystyle d_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}\right|\;,\;\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}=x_{0}^{2}-\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\right)^{2}=\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{2}-\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)^{2}\;.

(34)

The impact parameter
d_{\sigma}
is assumed to be larger or equal (grazing light ray) than the (equatorial) radius
P
of the massive Solar System body,

\displaystyle d_{\sigma}\geq P\;.

(35)

For graphical illustration see Figure
1
. From these definitions one obtains the relations

\displaystyle c\left(t-t_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)-\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\right),

(36)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}+\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\,,

(37)

\displaystyle\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{2}-\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}\,,

(38)

\displaystyle\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)^{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{4}-2\,\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{2}\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}+\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{4}\,,

(39)

which are frequently used in the subsequent considerations.

Figure 1:

A geometrical representation of the propagation of a light signal through the gravitational field of a massive Solar System body at rest.
The origin of the spatial coordinates
x^{1},x^{2},x^{3}
is assumed to be located at the center of mass of the body.
The light signal is emitted by the light source at
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}
in the direction of the unit vector
\mu
and propagates along the exact
light trajectory
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t\right)
in (
28
) which is determined by the geodesic equation (
19
).
The unit tangent vector
\sigma
and
\nu
of the light trajectory at past infinity and future infinity are
given by Eqs. (
24
) and (
95
), respectively.
The unit tangent vector
n
of the light trajectory at the observer’s position
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}
is defined by Eq. (
117
).
The unperturbed light ray
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\left(t\right)
is given by
Eq. (
31
) and propagates in the direction of
\sigma
along a straight line through the position
of the light source at
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}
. The impact vector
\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}
of the unperturbed light ray is given by Eq. (
33
).
The impact vector
\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}}
as given by Eq. (
130
) is parallel to
\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}
and a tiny bit smaller,

\hat{d}_{\sigma}<d_{\sigma}
, but is not shown in the diagram.

V
Light propagation in 1PN approximation

V.1
The 1PN metric for a body with monopole and quadrupole term

In our investigation, the body is assumed to be at rest and without rotational motion, hence the metric is time-independent.
Then, the post-Newtonian series expansion of the metric tensor reads

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)=\eta_{\alpha\beta}+h^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)+{\cal O}\left(c^{-4}\right),

(40)

where
h_{\alpha\beta}^{(2)}={\cal O}\left(c^{-2}\right)
and
x
is some field point.
These metric coefficients are given in terms of so-called symmetric trace-free (STF) mass-multipoles
\hat{M}_{L}
and spin-multipoles
\hat{S}_{L}

as given by Eqs. (5.33) and (5.35) in
Multipole_Damour_2
.
If one takes into account only the mass-monopole and mass-quadrupole terms, then the metric perturbation in 1PN approximation reads

Klioner1991
;
Klioner2003b
;
Zschocke_2PM_Metric

\displaystyle h^{\left(2\right)}_{00}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\,G}{c^{2}}\,\frac{M}{x}+\frac{3\,G\,\hat{M}_{ab}}{c^{2}}\,\frac{x^{a}\,x^{b}}{x^{5}}\;,

(41)

\displaystyle h^{\left(2\right)}_{ij}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h^{\left(2\right)}_{00}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)\,\delta_{ij}\;,

(42)

while
h^{\left(2\right)}_{0i}=0
and the mass-monopole and mass-quadrupole are given by Eqs. (
17
) and (
18
), respectively.
Let us notice that Eqs. (
41
) and (
42
) are identical
with Eqs. (1) and (2) in
Klioner1991
owing to the STF relation (cf. Eq. (1.158) in
Poisson_Will
)

\displaystyle M_{ab}\,\hat{x}^{ab}=\hat{M}_{ab}\,x^{ab}=\hat{M}_{ab}\,\hat{x}^{ab}\;,

(43)

where
M_{ab}=\hat{M}_{ab}+\delta_{ab}\,M_{kk}/3
.
For the same reason Eqs. (
41
) and (
42
) agree with Eqs. (145) and (147) in
Zschocke_2PM_Metric
.

V.2
The geodesic equation in 1PN approximation

Inserting the expansion (
40
) into
the geodesic equation (
19
) yields the geodesic equation in 1PN approximation,

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}^{i}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,h_{00,i}^{(2)}-h_{00,j}^{(2)}\frac{\dot{x}^{i}\left(t\right)}{c}\frac{\dot{x}^{j}\left(t\right)}{c}-h_{ij,k}^{(2)}\frac{\dot{x}^{j}\left(t\right)}{c}\frac{\dot{x}^{k}\left(t\right)}{c}+\frac{1}{2}\,h_{jk,i}^{(2)}\frac{\dot{x}^{j}\left(t\right)}{c}\frac{\dot{x}^{k}\left(t\right)}{c}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-4}\right),

(44)

where it has been taken into account that the space-time component of the metric perturbation
h^{\left(2\right)}_{0i}=0
vanishes for a body at rest.
In (
44
) the field point
x
as argument of the metric perturbations is taken at the position of the light signal, while

\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t\right)
and
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t\right)
are the coordinate velocity and spatial coordinate of the light signal at this field point.
For the geodesic equation in 1PN approximation, one may take the unperturbed light ray (
\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm N}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}
)
on the r.h.s. in (
44
).
In view of (
42
), the 1PN geodesic equation in (
44
) then simplifies considerably,

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}^{i}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}=h_{00,i}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)-2\,h_{00,j}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)\;\sigma^{i}\,\sigma^{j}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-4}\right)\,.

(45)

Determining the spatial derivatives of
h_{00}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)
in (
41
) and afterwards replacing
x
by
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}

(cf. (Eq.
21
)) one gets

\displaystyle h_{00,i}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\,\frac{GM}{c^{2}}\,\frac{x_{\rm N}^{i}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{3}}+6\,\frac{G\hat{M}_{ai}}{c^{2}}\,\frac{x_{\rm N}^{a}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{5}}-15\,\frac{G\hat{M}_{ab}}{c^{2}}\,\frac{x_{\rm N}^{a}x_{\rm N}^{b}x_{\rm N}^{i}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{7}}\;.

(46)

By inserting (
46
) into (
45
) one may separate terms proportional
to the monopole and quadrupole, and obtains

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}=\frac{\ddot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{\rm M}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-4}\right),

(47)

where the monopole and quadrupole terms are given by

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}^{i\,{\rm M}}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{GM}{c^{2}}\bigg[-2\,\frac{x_{\rm N}^{i}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{3}}+4\,\sigma^{i}\,\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{3}}\bigg],

(48)

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}^{i\,{\rm Q}}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{G\hat{M}_{ab}}{c^{2}}\bigg[6\,\delta^{bi}\,\frac{x_{\rm N}^{a}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{5}}-12\,\sigma^{i}\,\sigma^{b}\,\frac{x_{\rm N}^{a}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{5}}-15\,\frac{x_{\rm N}^{a}\,x_{\rm N}^{b}\,x_{\rm N}^{i}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{7}}+30\,\sigma^{i}\,\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{7}}\,x_{\rm N}^{a}\,x_{\rm N}^{b}\bigg],

(49)

which agrees with
Klioner1991
;
see also endnote
4
4

4

Let us note that the trace terms of the quadrupole moment
are not zero but they cancel each other in Eq. (26) in
Klioner1991
, while in (
49
) there are no trace terms at all.
.
By means of relations (
37
) and (
38
) the r.h.s. of equations (
48
)
and (
49
) can be rewritten in terms of time-independent tensorial coefficients and time-dependent
scalar functions,

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}^{i\,{\rm M}}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{GM}{c^{2}}\bigg[{\cal A}^{i}_{\left(3\right)}\,\ddot{\cal W}_{\left(3\right)}\left(t\right)+{\cal B}^{i}_{\left(3\right)}\,\ddot{\cal X}_{\left(3\right)}\left(t\right)\bigg],

(50)

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}^{i\,{\rm Q}}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{G\hat{M}_{ab}}{c^{2}}\!\!\sum\limits_{n=5,7}\!\bigg[{\cal C}^{i\,ab}_{\left(n\right)}\ddot{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)+{\cal D}^{i\,ab}_{\left(n\right)}\ddot{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)\!\bigg],

(51)

where the time-independent coefficients read

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{i}_{\left(3\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+2\,\sigma^{i}\;,

(52)

\displaystyle{\cal B}^{i}_{\left(3\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\,d_{\sigma}^{i}\;,

(53)

\displaystyle{\cal C}^{i\,ab}_{\left(5\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\,6\,\sigma^{a}\,\delta^{bi}+3\,\sigma^{a}\,\sigma^{b}\,\sigma^{i}\;,

(54)

\displaystyle{\cal C}^{i\,ab}_{\left(7\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-15\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}\sigma^{a}\sigma^{b}\sigma^{i}+15\,d^{a}_{\sigma}d^{b}_{\sigma}\sigma^{i}-30\,\sigma^{a}d^{b}_{\sigma}\,d^{i}_{\sigma}\;,

(55)

\displaystyle{\cal D}^{i\,ab}_{\left(5\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\,6\,d^{a}_{\sigma}\,\delta^{bi}-15\,\sigma^{a}\,\sigma^{b}\,d^{i}_{\sigma}+18\,\sigma^{a}\,d^{b}_{\sigma}\,\sigma^{i}\;,

(56)

\displaystyle{\cal D}^{i\,ab}_{\left(7\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-15\,d^{a}_{\sigma}d^{b}_{\sigma}d^{i}_{\sigma}+15\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}\sigma^{a}\sigma^{b}d^{i}_{\sigma}-30\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}\sigma^{a}d^{b}_{\sigma}\sigma^{i},

(57)

and the scalar functions are

\displaystyle\ddot{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{n}}\;,

(58)

\displaystyle\ddot{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{n}}\;.

(59)

In the following two subsections the first and second integration of differential equations (
50
)
and (
51
) will be performed.

V.3
The first integration of geodesic equation in 1PN approximation

The first integration of geodesic equation (
47
) yields the coordinate velocity of the light signal in 1PN approximation,

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t\right)}{c}=\int\limits_{-\infty}^{t}dc{\rm t}\,\frac{\ddot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left({\rm t}\right)}{c^{2}}=\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}+\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-4}\right).

(60)

By inserting (
50
) and (
51
) into the r.h.s. of
(
60
) one obtains the first integration of geodesic equation (coordinate velocity) for a light signal
in the gravitational field of one body at rest

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{\rm M}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c}+\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c}\;,

(61)

where their spatial components are

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta\dot{x}^{i\,{\rm M}}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{GM}{c^{2}}\bigg[{\cal A}^{i}_{\left(3\right)}\,\dot{\cal W}_{\left(3\right)}\left(t\right)+{\cal B}^{i}_{\left(3\right)}\,\dot{\cal X}_{\left(3\right)}\left(t\right)\bigg],

(62)

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta\dot{x}^{i\,{\rm Q}}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{G\hat{M}_{ab}}{c^{2}}\sum\limits_{n=5,7}\bigg[{\cal C}^{i\,ab}_{\left(n\right)}\,\dot{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)+{\cal D}^{i\,ab}_{\left(n\right)}\,\dot{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)\bigg].

(63)

The scalar master integrals
\dot{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}
and
\dot{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}
are defined by
Eqs. (
174
) and (
175
), respectively, while their explicit solutions for
n=3,5,7

are given by Eq. (
179
) and Eqs. (
184
), (
186
), (
188
)
in Appendix
D
.
One may show that Eq. (
62
) agrees with
Brumberg1991
and
Eq. (
63
) agrees with
Klioner1991
;
see also endnote
5
5

5

By means of
M_{ab}=\hat{M}_{ab}+\delta_{ab}\,M_{ii}/3
, one may recognize that the trace terms of the
quadrupole
moment cancel each other in the coefficients given by Eqs. (29) in
Klioner1991
.
.
It is easy to check that the solutions in (
60
) - (
63
) satisfy the
null condition (
20
) up to terms of the order
{\cal O}\left(c^{-4}\right)
.

V.4
The second integration of the geodesic equation in 1PN approximation

The second integration of geodesic equation (trajectory) for a light signal in the gravitational field of one body at rest endowed
with the mass-monopole and mass-quadrupole in the 1PN approximation reads

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{t_{0}}^{t}dc{\rm t}\,\frac{\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left({\rm t}\right)}{c}=\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}+\left(t-t_{0}\right)\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+{\cal O}\left(c^{-4}\right).

(64)

Inserting the r.h.s. of (
60
) with Eqs. (
61
) - (
63
)
into (
64
) yields

\displaystyle\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)=\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}^{\rm M}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right),

(65)

where their spatial components are

\displaystyle\Delta x^{i\,{\rm M}}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)=\frac{GM}{c^{2}}\bigg[{\cal A}^{i}_{\left(3\right)}\,{\cal W}_{\left(3\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+{\cal B}^{i}_{\left(3\right)}\,{\cal X}_{\left(3\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)\bigg],

(66)

\displaystyle\Delta x^{i\,{\rm Q}}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)=\frac{G\hat{M}_{ab}}{c^{2}}\sum\limits_{n=5,7}\bigg[{\cal C}^{iab}_{\left(n\right)}\,{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+{\cal D}^{iab}_{\left(n\right)}\,{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)\bigg].

(67)

The scalar master integrals
{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}
and
{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}
are defined by
Eqs. (
193
) and (
194
), respectively, while their explicit solutions for
n=3,5,7
are given
by Eqs. (
202
), (
204
), (
206
) and
Eqs. (
208
), (
210
), (
212
) in Appendix
D
.
One may show that Eq. (
66
) agrees with
Brumberg1991
and
Eq. (
67
) agrees with
Klioner1991
;
see also endnote
6
6

6

We note that
\displaystyle\ln\frac{x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{0}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}=-\ln\frac{x_{\rm N}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{0}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}
.
.

VI
Light propagation in 2PN approximation

VI.1
The 2PM metric for a body with monopole and quadrupole term

The post-Newtonian series expansion in 2PM approximation of the metric tensor reads

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)=\eta_{\alpha\beta}+h^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)+h^{(4)}_{\alpha\beta}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right).

(68)

The 1PN terms of the metric perturbation,
h^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}
, were given by Eqs. (
41
) - (
42
).
The 2PN terms of the metric perturbation,
h_{\alpha\beta}^{(4)}
, in canonical harmonic coordinates read
Zschocke_2PM_Metric

\displaystyle h^{\left(4\right)}_{00}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2\,G^{2}}{c^{4}}\,\frac{M^{2}}{x^{2}}-\frac{6\,G^{2}}{c^{4}}\frac{M\,\hat{M}_{ab}}{x^{4}}\hat{n}_{ab}-\frac{G^{2}}{c^{4}}\frac{\hat{M}_{ab}\,\hat{M}_{cd}}{x^{6}}\left(\frac{3}{5}\delta_{ac}\delta_{bd}+\frac{18}{7}\delta_{ac}\hat{n}_{bd}+\frac{9}{2}\hat{n}_{abcd}\right),

(69)

\displaystyle h^{\left(4\right)}_{ij}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{G^{2}}{c^{4}}\,\frac{M^{2}}{x^{2}}\left(\frac{4}{3}\,\delta_{ij}+\hat{n}_{ij}\right)+\frac{G^{2}\,M\,\hat{M}_{ab}}{c^{4}\;x^{4}}\left(\frac{15}{2}\,\hat{n}_{ijab}+\frac{32}{7}\,\delta_{ij}\,\hat{n}_{ab}-\frac{12}{7}\,\delta_{a\,(i}\hat{n}_{j\,)b}\right)

(70)

\displaystyle\hskip-21.33955pt+\frac{G^{2}}{c^{4}}\frac{\hat{M}_{ab}\,\hat{M}_{cd}}{x^{6}}\bigg(\frac{75}{4}\,\hat{n}_{ijabcd}-\frac{90}{11}\,\hat{n}_{ijac}\,\delta_{bd}+\frac{27}{11}\,\hat{n}_{abcd}\,\delta_{ij}-\frac{25}{84}\,\hat{n}_{ij}\,\delta_{ac}\,\delta_{bd}+\frac{83}{42}\,\hat{n}_{ad}\,\delta_{bc}\,\delta_{ij}+\frac{16}{35}\,\delta_{ac}\,\delta_{bd}\,\delta_{ij}

\displaystyle\hskip-21.33955pt+\frac{18}{11}\,\hat{n}_{acd(i}\,\delta_{j)b}-\frac{5}{21}\,\hat{n}_{a(i}\,\delta_{j)c}\,\delta_{bd}+\frac{10}{21}\,\delta_{ci}\,\delta_{dj}\,\hat{n}_{ab}-\frac{23}{42}\,\delta_{b(i}\,\delta_{j)c}\,\hat{n}_{ad}-\frac{6}{35}\,\delta_{ad}\,\delta_{b(i}\,\delta_{j)c}\bigg),

while
h^{\left(4\right)}_{0i}=0
.
The STF tensors
\hat{n}_{L}
are given by Eq. (
161
);
for the explicit form of
\hat{n}_{ab}
and
\hat{n}_{abcd}
as well as
\hat{n}_{abcde\!f}
see Eqs. (
162
) and (
163
)
as well as (
164
) in Appendix
B
.
The symmetrization operation is defined by Eq. (
156
) in Appendix
B
and
x=\left|\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right|
is the
absolute of the spatial coordinates of some field point where the origin of the coordinate system is located at the center of mass of the body.

VI.2
The geodesic equation in 2PN approximation

The geodesic equation in 2PN approximation has been given, for instance, in
Brumberg1991
as well as in several articles,
e.g.
Bruegmann2005
;
Zschocke3
;
Zschocke4
. Inserting (
68
) into the geodesic equation (
19
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}^{i}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\,h_{00,i}^{(2)}-h_{00,j}^{(2)}\frac{\dot{x}^{i}\left(t\right)}{c}\frac{\dot{x}^{j}\left(t\right)}{c}-h_{ij,k}^{(2)}\frac{\dot{x}^{j}\left(t\right)}{c}\frac{\dot{x}^{k}\left(t\right)}{c}+\frac{1}{2}\,h_{jk,i}^{(2)}\frac{\dot{x}^{j}\left(t\right)}{c}\frac{\dot{x}^{k}\left(t\right)}{c}-\frac{1}{2}\,h_{ij}^{(2)}h_{00,j}^{(2)}-h_{00}^{(2)}h_{00,j}^{(2)}\frac{\dot{x}^{i}\left(t\right)}{c}\frac{\dot{x}^{j}\left(t\right)}{c}

(71)

\displaystyle\hskip-35.56593pt+h_{is}^{(2)}h_{sj,k}^{(2)}\frac{\dot{x}^{j}\left(t\right)}{c}\frac{\dot{x}^{k}\left(t\right)}{c}-\frac{1}{2}h_{is}^{(2)}h_{jk,s}^{(2)}\frac{\dot{x}^{j}\left(t\right)}{c}\frac{\dot{x}^{k}\left(t\right)}{c}+\frac{1}{2}h_{00,i}^{(4)}-h_{00,j}^{(4)}\frac{\dot{x}^{i}\left(t\right)}{c}\frac{\dot{x}^{j}\left(t\right)}{c}-h_{ij,k}^{(4)}\frac{\dot{x}^{j}\left(t\right)}{c}\frac{\dot{x}^{k}\left(t\right)}{c}+\frac{1}{2}h_{jk,i}^{(4)}\frac{\dot{x}^{j}\left(t\right)}{c}\frac{\dot{x}^{k}\left(t\right)}{c}

\displaystyle\hskip-35.56593pt+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right)\,.

In (
71
) the field point
x
as argument of the metric perturbations is taken at the position of the light signal, while

\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t\right)
and
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t\right)
are the coordinate velocity and spatial coordinate of the light signal at this field point.
For the geodesic equation in 2PN approximation,
one has to take the 1PN light ray (
\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 1PN}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}
) for the first four terms on the r.h.s. in (
71
)
and the unperturbed light ray (
\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm N}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}
) for the other terms on the r.h.s. in (
71
).
Accordingly, in view of (
42
) and (
60
), the 2PM geodesic equation in (
71
) simplifies considerably,

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}^{i}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+h_{00,i}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\right)-2\,h_{00,j}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\right)\,\sigma^{i}\sigma^{j}+h_{00,i}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)\,\frac{\sigma^{j}\Delta\dot{x}^{j}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)}{c}-2\,h_{00,j}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)\,\frac{\Delta\dot{x}^{j}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)}{c}\,\sigma^{i}

(72)

\displaystyle\hskip-39.83368pt-2\,h_{00,j}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)\,\frac{\Delta\dot{x}^{i}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)}{c}\,\sigma^{j}-\,h_{00}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)\,h_{00,i}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\,h_{00,i}^{(4)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)-h_{00,j}^{(4)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)\,\sigma^{i}\sigma^{j}-h_{ij,k}^{(4)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)\,\sigma^{j}\sigma^{k}

\displaystyle\hskip-39.83368pt+\frac{1}{2}\,h_{jk,i}^{(4)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)\,\sigma^{j}\sigma^{k}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right),

whereby the spatial derivatives are to be understood that one has to perform first these derivatives and afterwards
one has to insert the light ray in the given approximation (cf. Eq. (
21
)). This equation consists of
10
individual terms,

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}_{A}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{B}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{C}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{D}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{E}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{F}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{G}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{H}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{I}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{J}^{i}}{c^{2}}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right),

(73)

as defined by Eqs. (
217
) - (
226
). These individual terms are given by Eqs. (
231
), (
),
(
234
), (
235
), (
236
), (

), (
238
), (

),
(
240
), (

) in Appendix
E
. Similar to (

), the sum of these terms in
(
73
) can be separated into monopole and quadrupole and mixed monopole-quadrupole terms,

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{\rm M}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{{\rm Q}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t\right)}{c^{2}}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right).

(74)

The 1PN terms
\ddot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{\rm M}_{\rm 1PN}
and

G^{1}

proportional to

VI.3
The first integration of geodesic equation in 2PN approximation

The first integration of geodesic equation (
73
) yields the coordinate velocity of the light signal in 2PN approximation,

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t\right)}{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{-\infty}^{t}dc{\rm t}\,\frac{\ddot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left({\rm t}\right)}{c^{2}}

(80)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}+\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c}+\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t\right)}{c}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right).

By inserting (
74
) into the r.h.s. of (
80
) one gets

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{\rm M}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c}+\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t\right)}{c}\;,

(81)

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t\right)}{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t\right)}{c}+\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t\right)}{c}+\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{{\rm Q}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t\right)}{c}\;.

(82)

The 1PN terms
\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{\rm M}_{\rm 1PN}
and
\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}
have been determined
by Eqs. (
62
) and (
63
), respectively, and will not be represented here again.

The first integration of the 2PN monopole-monopole term in (
75
) is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta\dot{x}^{i\,{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t\right)}{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\bigg[\sum\limits_{n=3}^{6}{\cal E}^{i}_{\left(n\right)}\;\dot{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)+\sum\limits_{n=2}^{6}{\cal F}^{i}_{\left(n\right)}\;\dot{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)+{\cal G}^{i}_{\left(5\right)}\;\dot{\cal Y}_{\left(5\right)}\left(t\right)+\sum\limits_{n=3,5}{\cal H}^{i}_{\left(n\right)}\;\dot{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)\bigg].

(83)

The first integration of the 2PN monopole-quadrupole term in (
78
) is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta\dot{x}^{i\,{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t\right)}{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{GM}{c^{2}}\,\frac{G\hat{M}_{ab}}{c^{2}}\bigg[\sum\limits_{n=3}^{10}{\cal K}^{i\,ab}_{\left(n\right)}\,\dot{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)+\sum\limits_{n=2}^{10}{\cal L}^{i\,ab}_{\left(n\right)}\,\dot{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)+\,\sum\limits_{n=7}^{9}{\cal M}^{i\,ab}_{\left(n\right)}\,\dot{\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)+\sum\limits_{n=5}^{9}{\cal N}^{i\,ab}_{\left(n\right)}\,\dot{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)\bigg].

(84)

The first integration of the 2PN quadrupole-quadrupole term in (
79
) is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta\dot{x}^{i\,{\rm Q}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t\right)}{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{G\hat{M}_{ab}}{c^{2}}\,\frac{G\hat{M}_{cd}}{c^{2}}\bigg[\sum\limits_{n=5}^{14}{\cal P}^{i\,abcd}_{\left(n\right)}\,\dot{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)+\sum\limits_{n=4}^{14}{\cal Q}^{i\,abcd}_{\left(n\right)}\,\dot{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)\bigg].

(85)

The master integrals
\dot{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}
,
\dot{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}
,
\dot{\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}
,
\dot{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}

are defined by Eqs. (
174
) - (
177
).
The solution for the master integral
\dot{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}
is given explicitly by Eq. (
179
),
while the solutions for the master integrals
\dot{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}
,

\dot{\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}
,

can be given in closed form by the recurrence relations
in Eqs. (

) - (

) in Appendix
D
.
The explicit form of
\dot{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}
,
\dot{\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}
,
\dot{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}

for the first few orders in
n
are given by Eqs. (
183
) - (
) in Appendix
D
.

Two comments are in order:
First of all, one may show, that

c\,\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}+\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{{\rm M}}_{\rm 1PN}+\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}
agrees with Eq. (3.2.37)
in
Brumberg1991
as well as with Eq. (44) in our article
Article_Zschocke1
;
see also endnote
7
7

7

In order to demonstrate that agreement, one has to perform a series expansion of
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}
which appears as
argument in the
vectorial functions in
Brumberg1991
;
Article_Zschocke1
(cf. Eqs. (
227
) - (
229
) in Appendix
E
).
.
And second, it has been checked numerically by the computer algebra system
Maple

Maple
that
the solutions in (
80
) - (
85
)
satisfy the null condition (
20
) along the entire light trajectory, which represents an independent
strict confirmation about the correctness of the time-independent coefficients given in Appendix
E
.

VI.4
The second integration of geodesic equation in 2PN approximation

The second integration of geodesic equation (
73
) yields the trajectory of the light signal in 2PN approximation,

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{-\infty}^{t}dc{\rm t}\,\frac{\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left({\rm t}\right)}{c}

(86)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}+c\left(t-t_{0}\right)\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right).

By inserting (
80
) with (
81
) and (
82
)
into the r.h.s. of (
86
) one gets

\displaystyle\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}^{\rm M}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right),

(87)

\displaystyle\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}^{{\rm Q}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right).

(88)

The 1PN terms
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}^{\rm M}_{\rm 1PN}
and
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}
have been determined
by Eqs. (
66
) and (
67
), respectively, and will not be represented here again.

The second integration of the 2PN monopole-monopole term in (
75
) is given by

\displaystyle\Delta x^{i\,{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\bigg[\sum\limits_{n=3}^{6}{\cal E}^{i}_{\left(n\right)}\,{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+\sum\limits_{n=2}^{6}{\cal F}^{i}_{\left(n\right)}\,{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+{\cal G}^{i}_{\left(5\right)}\,{\cal Y}_{\left(5\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+\sum\limits_{n=3,5}{\cal H}^{i}_{\left(n\right)}\,{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)\bigg].

(89)

The second integration of the 2PN monopole-quadrupole term in (
78
) is given by

\displaystyle\Delta x^{i\,{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{GM}{c^{2}}\,\frac{G\hat{M}_{ab}}{c^{2}}\bigg[\sum\limits_{n=3}^{10}{\cal K}^{i\,ab}_{\left(n\right)}\,{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+\sum\limits_{n=2}^{10}{\cal L}^{i\,ab}_{\left(n\right)}\,{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+\sum\limits_{n=7}^{9}{\cal M}^{i\,ab}_{\left(n\right)}\,{\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+\sum\limits_{n=5}^{9}{\cal N}^{i\,ab}_{\left(n\right)}\,{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)\bigg].

The second integration of the 2PN quadrupole-quadrupole term in (
79
) is given by

\displaystyle\Delta x^{i\,{\rm Q}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(t,t_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{G\hat{M}_{ab}}{c^{2}}\,\frac{G\hat{M}_{cd}}{c^{2}}\bigg[\sum\limits_{n=5}^{14}{\cal P}^{i\,abcd}_{\left(n\right)}\,{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)+\sum\limits_{n=4}^{14}{\cal Q}^{i\,abcd}_{\left(n\right)}\,{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)\bigg].

(91)

The master integrals
{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}
,
{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}
,
{\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}
,
{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}
are defined by
Eqs. (
193
) - (
196
).
The solutions for the master integrals
{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}
,
{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}
,

{\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}
,
{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}
can be given in closed form by the recurrence relations
in Eqs. (
198
) - (
201
) in Appendix
D
.
The explicit form of
{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}
,
{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}
,
{\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}
,
{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}

for the first few orders in
n
are given by Eqs. (
202
) - (
) in Appendix
D
.

One comment is in order:
One may show, that

\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}^{{\rm M}}_{\rm 1PN}+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}
agrees with Eq. (3.2.38) in
Brumberg1991

as well as with Eq. (45) in our article
Article_Zschocke1
.
After some algebraic manipulations, mainly using relation (
34
), one may also demonstrate that (
89
)
agrees with Eq. (J11) in
Zschocke2
.

The presented solutions of the first integration (
80
) and second integration (
86
)
of geodesic equation are strictly valid for any astrometric configuration between light source, body and observer,
that means for
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\leq 0
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}>0
as well as for

\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\leq 0
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}>0
.

VII
The total light deflection

The solution of the first integration of the initial value problem allows to determine the total light deflection.
For that, one has to consider the unit tangent vectors along the light trajectory at
t\rightarrow\pm\infty
,

\sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t\right)}{c}\bigg|_{t=-\infty}\;\;,

(92)

\nu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t\right)}{c}\bigg|_{t=+\infty}\;\;.

(93)

The total light deflection is defined as angle between these unit tangent vectors,
\delta\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$},\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}\right)
,
and the sine of total light deflection angle is given by

\displaystyle\sin\delta\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$},\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}\right|\,.

(94)

From (
80
) - (
82
) with (
62
) - (
63
) and
(
83
) - (
85
) one obtains for the unit tangent vector
\nu
in (
93
):

\nu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}+\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{\rm M}_{\rm 1PN}+\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}+\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}+\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}+\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{{\rm Q}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\;,

(95)

\displaystyle\nu_{\rm 1PN}^{i\,{\rm M}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{GM}{c^{2}}\left[{\cal B}^{i}_{\left(3\right)}\,\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(3\right)}\right],

(96)

\displaystyle\nu_{\rm 1PN}^{i\,{\rm Q}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{G\hat{M}_{ab}}{c^{2}}\left[\sum\limits_{n=5,7}{\cal D}^{i\,ab}_{\left(n\right)}\;\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(n\right)}\right],

(97)

\displaystyle\nu_{\rm 2PN}^{i\,{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\left[\sum\limits_{n=2}^{6}{\cal F}^{i}_{\left(n\right)}\,\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(n\right)}+{\cal G}^{i}_{\left(5\right)}\,\dot{\cal Y}^{\infty}_{\left(5\right)}\right],

(98)

\displaystyle\nu_{\rm 2PN}^{i\,{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{GM}{c^{2}}\,\frac{G\hat{M}_{ab}}{c^{2}}\left[\sum\limits_{n=2}^{10}{\cal L}^{i\,ab}_{\left(n\right)}\;\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(n\right)}+\sum\limits_{n=7}^{9}{\cal M}^{i\,ab}_{\left(n\right)}\;\dot{\cal Y}^{\infty}_{\left(n\right)}\right],

(99)

\displaystyle\nu_{\rm 2PN}^{i\,{\rm Q}\times{\rm Q}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{G\hat{M}_{ab}}{c^{2}}\,\frac{G\hat{M}_{cd}}{c^{2}}\left[\sum\limits_{n=4}^{14}{\cal Q}^{i\,abcd}_{\left(n\right)}\;\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(n\right)}\right],

(100)

where the abbreviations

\displaystyle\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(n\right)}=\dot{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)\bigg|_{t=+\infty}\;{\rm and}\quad\dot{\cal Y}^{\infty}_{\left(n\right)}=\dot{\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)\bigg|_{t=+\infty}

(101)

have been introduced. It has been checked analytically that
\nu
in (
95
) is in fact a unit vector,

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}=1+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right).

(102)

The result (
102
) demonstrates that the null condition (
20
) is satisfied along the light ray at
future infinity up to terms of the order
{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right)
(note that the metric perturbations
h^{\left(n\right)}_{\alpha\beta}
vanish at
infinite distances from the body according to Eq. (
9
)). Furthermore,
the proof of (
102
) implies an independent check about the correctness of those time-independent coefficients which arise in (
95
).

In (
96
) - (
100
) it has been taken into account that in the limit
t\rightarrow+\infty
all terms
with
\dot{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}
vanish, while all terms with
\dot{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}
cancel each other.
The limits in (
101
) are given by Eqs. (
192
) in Appendix
D
.

From (
95
) one obtains for the three-vector of total light deflection

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{\rm M}_{\rm 1PN}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{{\rm Q}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\;,

(103)

where the spatial components of the individual terms of the three-vector
\nu
are given by Eqs. (
96
) - (
100
).
The total light deflection (
103
) is a coordinate-independent quantity and irrespective that it
is calculated in a concrete coordinate system it is, in principle, an observable. The absolute value of the total light deflection
(
103
) can be estimated by

\displaystyle\hskip-21.33955pt\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}\big|\leq\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{\rm M}_{\rm 1PN}\big|+\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}\big|+\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}\big|+\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\big|+\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{{\rm Q}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\big|\;.

(104)

In order to determine the upper limit of each individual term in (
104
) the meaningful assumption is adopted,
that to a good approximation the giant planets can be considered as axially symmetric bodies, where the STF quadrupole tensor (
18
) takes
the following form
Klioner2003b
;
Zschocke6
:

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{ab}=M\,J_{2}\,P^{2}\,\frac{1}{3}\;{\cal R}\;{\rm diag}\left(+1,+1,-2\right)\,{\cal R}^{\rm T}\;,

(105)

where
{\cal R}
is the rotational matrix giving the orientation of the unit-vector
{\mbox{\boldmath$e$}}_{3}
of the symmetry axis of the massive body in the coordinate system,

M
is the mass of the body,
J_{2}
is the actual second zonal harmonic coefficient, and
P
is the equatorial radius of the body.
The parameters in (
105
) are given in Table
1
for the giant planets of the Solar System.

By inserting (

) into (

) - (

) one obtains (details are given in
Appendices

-

):

\displaystyle\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{\rm M}_{\rm 1PN}\big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\,\frac{GM}{c^{2}}\,\frac{1}{d_{\sigma}}\;,

(106)

\displaystyle\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}\big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\,\frac{GM}{c^{2}}\,\left|J_{2}\right|\,\frac{P^{2}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}\;,

(107)

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\left|\frac{15}{4}\,\frac{\pi}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}-8\,\frac{x_{0}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}\right|\,,

(108)

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\,\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\,\left|J_{2}\right|\,\frac{P^{2}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\,\left|\frac{15}{4}\,\frac{\pi}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}+8\,\frac{x_{0}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}\right|\,,

(109)

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{{\rm Q}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\,\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\,\left|J_{2}\right|^{2}\,\frac{P^{4}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{4}}\,\left|\frac{15}{4}\,\frac{\pi}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}+12\,\frac{x_{0}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}\right|\,.

(110)

These upper limits are strictly valid for any astrometric configuration between source, body and observer, that means for

\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\leq 0
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}>0
as well as for
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\leq 0
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}>0
.
The upper limits (
106
) - (
107
)
take their maximal value in case of a light ray grazing at the surface of the massive body (
d_{\sigma}=P_{A}
). These maximal numerical values
are presented in Table

for the giant planets of the Solar System.

If the massive body is, so to speak, located between the light source and the observer, then the following condition is valid,

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 0\;,

(111)

a condition that applies with certainty for any stellar light source.
Then, the upper limits of the 2PN terms (

) - (

) simplify as follows

\displaystyle\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}\big|

(112)

\displaystyle\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\big|

\displaystyle\leq

) - (

) take their maximal value in case

). These maximal numerical values
for such configurations are presented in Table
