In view of the small numerical values in Table
3
one might be inclined to believe that the light deflection caused by the quadrupole structure
of any massive Solar System body is irrelevant in second post-Newtonian approximation for future astrometry missions on the sub-micro-arcsecond level and
even on the nas-level of accuracy. This is, however, not true, because of the occurrence of so-called
enhanced 2PN terms
, which will be considered
in the following Section.

VIII
Enhanced terms

In the previous Section it has been shown that in case of condition (
111
) the 2PN terms in the total light deflection
in Eqs. (
108
) - (
110
) can be estimated as
\displaystyle{\rm const.}\times\frac{m^{2}}{d_{\sigma}^{2}}
.
Therefore, they are so tiny that they can be neglected even
for high-precision astrometry on the nano-arcsecond level; their numerical values are given in Table
3
for grazing light rays (
d_{\sigma}=P
).
However, as worked out in several investigations
Article_Zschocke1
;
Teyssandier
;
AshbyBertotti2010
there are so-called
enhanced terms
of
second post-Newtonian order in the theory of light propagation, whose upper limits are given by

\displaystyle{\rm const.}\times\frac{m^{2}}{d_{\sigma}^{2}}\,\frac{x_{1}}{d_{\sigma}}
, where
x_{1}
is the distance between massive body and observer
and
d_{\sigma}
is the impact vector of the light ray. Hence
\displaystyle\frac{x_{1}}{d_{\sigma}}\gg 1
for grazing rays where the impact vector equals
the radius of the body. It is emphasized that these enhanced terms occur in case of condition (
111
), which is, for instance, always valid for
light sources far away from the Solar System, like stellar light sources.

The occurrence of
enhanced terms
becomes apparent when the boundary value problem of light propagation is considered instead the initial value problem.
Let
\left(ct_{0},\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\right)
be the four-coordinates of the light source at the moment of emission of the light signal, and
\left(ct_{1},\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)
be
the four-coordinates of the observer at the moment of reception of the light signal. Then, the boundary value problem of light propagation is determined as
solution of geodesic equation (
19
) with two boundary conditions

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t\right)\,\,\bigg|_{t=t_{0}}\;,

(115)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t\right)\,\,\bigg|_{t=t_{1}}\;.

(116)

In reality an observer will not be located at infinity but at some finite distance from the massive Solar System body. Therefore, for relativistic modeling of
real astrometric observations it is not sufficient to determine the initial value problem, defined by Eqs. (
23
) and (
24
),
but the solution of the boundary value problem, defined by Eqs. (
115
) and (
116
), is required.
This fact implies that for real astrometric measurements not the unit tangent-vector

\nu
at plus infinity is relevant, but the unit tangent-vector
n
at the time of reception,
t_{1}
, that means at the exact position
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}

of the observer (cf. Fig.
1
). This unit tangent-vector is defined by

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$n$}=\frac{\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t_{1}\right)}{\left|\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t_{1}\right)\right|}\;,

(117)

and the sine of the light deflection angle
\delta\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$},\mbox{\boldmath$n$}\right)
at the observer’s position is given by

\displaystyle\sin\delta\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$},\mbox{\boldmath$n$}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$n$}\right|.

(118)

The unit tangent-vector (
117
) is of fundamental importance for astrometry and implies that one has to introduce the impact vector

\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}}
defined below by Eq. (
130
). Therefore, this impact vector becomes indispensable for modeling of real
astrometric measurements and we will see that
\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}}
is the reason for the occurrence of
enhanced terms

in the 2PN light deflection.

Now we consider the unit vector in (
117
). Inserting (
80
) into (
117
) yields for this vector in the 2PN approximation

n

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}+\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)+\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right),

(119)

where
\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 1PN}={\cal O}\left(c^{-2}\right)
and
\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}={\cal O}\left(c^{-4}\right)
, which read

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 1PN}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 1PN}}{c}-\frac{\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 1PN}\right)}{c}\,\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\;,

(120)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 2PN}}{c}-\frac{\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 2PN}\right)}{c}\,\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}-\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 1PN}}{c}\!\left(\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 1PN}}{c}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\!\left(\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 1PN}}{c}\cdot\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 1PN}}{c}\right)\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}+\frac{3}{2}\!\left(\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 1PN}}{c}\right)^{2}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$},

(121)

where
\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 1PN}
is given by Eqs. (
61
) - (
63
), while

\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 2PN}
is given by Eqs. (
82
) - (
85
).

The 1PN terms
\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 1PN}
and the 2PN terms
\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}
in (
119
) depend on the spatial coordinates of the light source
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}

as well as on the spatial coordinates of the unperturbed light signal at the moment of reception:
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\left(t_{1}\right)
. However, for the boundary
value problem the solution of the unit tangent-vector
n
must be given in terms of the position of the observer
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}
. But only in the 2PN terms

\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}
in (
119
) the argument
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\left(t_{1}\right)
can be replaced by the spatial position of the observer
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}

because of

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}=\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\left(t_{1}\right)+{\cal O}\left(c^{-2}\right),

(122)

hence such a replacement in
\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}
would cause an error of the order
{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right)
in Eq. (
119
)
which is in line with the 2PN approximation. However, the argument
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\left(t_{1}\right)
of the 1PN terms
\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 1PN}

in (
119
) can not be replaced by
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}
, because such a replacement
would cause an error of the order
{\cal O}\left(c^{-4}\right)
in (
119
) which would spoil the 2PN approximation.
Therefore, one has to rewrite
\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 1PN}
in Eq. (
119
) in such a form that it’s argument is not
the spatial coordinate of the unperturbed light signal,
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\left(t_{1}\right)
, but given by the spatial coordinate of the
light signal in 1PN approximation (
64
),
that means
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t_{1}\right)=\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\left(t_{1}\right)+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t_{1}\right)
,
where
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}
is given by Eqs. (
65
) - (
67
).
Then Eq. (
119
) reads

n

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}+\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\right)+\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right),

(123)

where
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}
is defined by

\displaystyle\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)=\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)-\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\right)+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right).

(124)

The expression for
\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)
and
\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\right)
are given
by Eqs. (
336
) and (
338
) in Appendix
J
in its explicit form.
Now, in Eq. (
123
) it is allowed to replace the argument in
\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\right)
by the exact spatial position of the
observer
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}
, because of

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}=\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t_{1}\right)+{\cal O}\left(c^{-4}\right),

(125)

which would cause an error of the order
{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right)
in (
123
) and would be in line with the 2PN approximation.
To be consistent with the 2PN approximation, the additional term
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}
in (

) has to be series expanded up to terms of the
order
{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right)
. The additional term
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}
is of second post-Newtonian order
{\cal O}\left(c^{-4}\right)

and, in view of relation (
122
), in
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}
one may replace
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\left(t_{1}\right)
by
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}
, that means
the spatial position of the unperturbed light ray at the time of reception by the exact spatial position of the observer.
Then, the unit tangent vector at the observers position follows from (
123
)

n

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}+\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)+\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right).

(126)

Let us consider each individual term in (
126
).
The expression
\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)
in (

) follows from (

) when

\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t_{1}\right)
is replaced by
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}
. Then, by inserting the multipole tensor (

(

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{\rm M}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)+\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right),

(127)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{\rm M}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\,\frac{GM}{c^{2}}\left(1+\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}}{x_{1}}\right)\frac{\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}}}{\left(\hat{d}_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\;,

(128)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\,\frac{GM}{c^{2}}\,J_{2}\,\frac{P^{2}}{\left(\hat{d}_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}\left(1+\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}}{x_{1}}\right)\left[\!\left(1-\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$e$}_{3}\right)^{2}-4\frac{\left(\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$e$}_{3}\right)^{2}}{\left(\hat{d}_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\right)\frac{\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}}}{\hat{d}_{\sigma}}+2\,\frac{\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$e$}_{3}}{\hat{d}_{\sigma}}\,\mbox{\boldmath$e$}_{3}\right]

(129)

\displaystyle+\,{\cal O}\left(\frac{GM}{c^{2}}\,\frac{J_{2}}{\hat{d}_{\sigma}}\,\frac{P^{2}}{\left(x_{1}\right)^{2}}\right).

In (
129
) terms proportional to
\sigma
have been omitted because they do not contribute to the light deflection angle in
(
118
), while terms proportional to

\displaystyle\frac{P^{2}}{\left(x_{1}\right)^{2}}\ll 1
have been neglected because they turn out to be much

1\,{\rm nas}
.
In the impact vector (
339
) the spatial coordinate of the light signal in 1PN approximation
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(t_{1}\right)

can be replaced by the exact position of the observer
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}
because of relation (

).
Thus, the impact vector in (

) - (

) can be written in the form

\displaystyle\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\right)\right)\quad{\rm and}\quad\hat{d}_{\sigma}=\left|\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}}\right|

(130)

up to terms of the order
{\cal O}\left(c^{-4}\right)
which would cause an error of the order

in (

)
and (

) in line with the 2PN approximation; note that
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}}=0
.
The impact parameter
\hat{d}_{\sigma}
is also larger than or equal to the equatorial radius
P
of the massive Solar System body

\displaystyle\hat{d}_{\sigma}\geq P\;.

(131)

The expression of the additional terms
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)
in (

) is given by
(see Eqs. (

\displaystyle\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}^{{\rm Q}\times{\rm Q}}\;,

(132)

\displaystyle\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\,\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\,\frac{1}{\left(\hat{d}_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(1+\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}}{x_{1}}\right)\frac{x_{1}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}-x_{0}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}{\hat{d}_{\sigma}}\frac{\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}}}{\hat{d}_{\sigma}}\,,

(133)

\displaystyle\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 16\,\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\,J_{2}\,\frac{P^{2}}{\left(\hat{d}_{\sigma}\right)^{4}}\left(1+\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}}{x_{1}}\right)\frac{x_{1}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}-x_{0}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}{\hat{d}_{\sigma}}

(134)

\displaystyle\times\Bigg[\left(1-\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$e$}_{3}\right)^{2}-3\,\bigg(\frac{\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$e$}_{3}}{\hat{d}_{\sigma}}\bigg)^{2}\right)\frac{\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}}}{\hat{d}_{\sigma}}+\frac{\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$e$}_{3}}{\hat{d}_{\sigma}}\;\mbox{\boldmath$e$}_{3}\Bigg],

\displaystyle\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}^{{\rm Q}\times{\rm Q}}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 12\,\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\,\left(J_{2}\right)^{2}\,\frac{P^{4}}{\left(\hat{d}_{\sigma}\right)^{6}}\left(1+\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}}{x_{1}}\right)\frac{x_{1}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}-x_{0}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}{\hat{d}_{\sigma}}\left(1-\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$e$}_{3}\right)^{2}\right)^{2}\,\frac{\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}}}{\hat{d}_{\sigma}}\;,

(135)

where in (
134
) a term proportional to
\sigma
has been omitted because it does not contribute to the light deflection
(
118
). In what follows it is shown that these terms are the so-called
enhanced 2PN terms
, because they are
proportional to the large factor
\displaystyle\frac{x_{1}}{\hat{d}_{\sigma}}
, where

is the distance between body and observer and

\hat{d}_{\sigma}
equals the radius of the body for grazing light rays.

In Appendix

it is shown that these
enhanced 2PN terms
in Eqs. (

) - (

)
are solely caused by the new impact vector (
130
) which is indispensable for modeling of real
astrometric observations; cf. text below Eq. (

) in Appendix
J
.
These expressions are obtained from Eqs. (
345
) - (
347
) by
replacing the spatial coordinate of the unperturbed light ray
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\left(t_{1}\right)
by the spatial coordinates of the observer
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}
. Such
replacement causes an error of the order
{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right)
, hence is in line with the 2PN approximation.
We notice that in (

) - (

) the impact vector
\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}
has been replaced by
\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}}

which is in line with the 2PN approximation, because of

\displaystyle\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-2}\right)

(136)

which results from (
122
).
Finally, the term
\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)

) is formally given by Eq. (
121
),
when
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\left(t_{1}\right)
is replaced by
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}
.

By inspection of the unit vector in (
126
), one obtains for the upper limit of the light deflection (

\displaystyle\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$n$}\big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)\big|+\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)\big|+\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)\big|+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right).

(137)

Then, by using (

\displaystyle\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$n$}\big|

\displaystyle\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{\rm M}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)\big|+\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)\big|+|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}\!\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)\!\big|+|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\!\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)\!\big|+|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{{\rm Q}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\!\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)\!\big|

(138)

\displaystyle+\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)\big|+\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)\big|+\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{{\rm Q}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\right)\big|+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right).

The 2PN terms in the second line of (
138
) are given by Eqs. (

) - (

)
in Appendix

. The upper limits of each individual term in Eq. (

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{\rm M}_{\rm 1PN}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\,\frac{GM}{c^{2}}\,\frac{1}{\hat{d}_{\sigma}}\;,

(139)

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\left|\frac{15}{4}\,\frac{\pi}{\left(\hat{d}_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}-8\,\frac{x_{0}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}{\left(\hat{d}_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}\right|,

(141)

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\,\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\,\left|J_{2}\right|\,\frac{P^{2}}{\left(\hat{d}_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\,\left|\frac{15}{4}\,\frac{\pi}{\left(\hat{d}_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}+8\,\frac{x_{0}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}{\left(\hat{d}_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}\right|,

(146)

These upper limits are strictly valid for any astrometric configuration, that means for
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\leq 0
and

\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}>0
as well as for
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\leq 0
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}>0
.

The estimation for the 1PN terms in (

) - (

) are not complicated; for instance

, while the upper limits for the 2PN terms

The upper limits for the
enhanced terms
in (
144
) - (

)
follow straightforward from their expressions in (

) - (

) by using the relation (see endnote

8
8

8

If
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\leq 0
then
\displaystyle x_{0}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\leq\frac{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}{x_{0}}\leq d_{\sigma}

which follows from
(
323
) with the minus-sign; note that
1-\sqrt{1-a^{2}}\leq a^{2}
for
0\leq a\leq 1
.
If
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}>0
then
x_{0}\leq x_{1}

which follows from geometrical considerations (light signal must reach the observer’s position). These facts allow us to show
(
147
).
)

\displaystyle 0\leq x_{1}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}-x_{0}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\leq x_{1}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\;,

(147)

which is strictly valid for any astrometric configuration, that means for
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\leq 0
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}>0

as well as
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\leq 0
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}>0
.
Furthermore, for the upper limit (

) relation (

) has been applied.

Now let us consider the
enhanced terms
in (
144
) - (
146
). These terms are proportional to
the large factor
\displaystyle\frac{x_{1}}{\hat{d_{\sigma}}}
, but this fact does not imply that these terms are divergent because their validity is restricted
to the case of realistic observers located in the Solar System or at least having a distance of less than several hundred
{\rm au}
from the massive Solar System
body (see text below Eq. (

)).

The
enhanced term
in (
144
) has already been obtained
in case of 2PN light propagation in the monopole field some time ago
Article_Zschocke1
;
Teyssandier
;
AshbyBertotti2010
;
e.g. relation (100) in
Article_Zschocke1
or relation(66) in
Teyssandier
as well as Section
9
in
AshbyBertotti2010
.
The estimations in (
145
) and (
146
) are new results which
demonstrate that
enhanced terms
do also arise in the case of 2PN light propagation in the quadrupole field.
As mentioned, these
enhanced terms
originate
because the light deflection in (
118
) is expressed in terms of the new impact vector (
130
).

There is an important difference between the 2PN terms in (
141
) - (
143
) and the

enhanced 2PN terms
in (
144
) - (
146
).
Namely, if one considers again the most relevant astrometric configurations, where the body is, so to speak, located between the light source and the observer,
that means where the condition (
111
) holds, then the 2PN terms in (
141
) - (
143
) simplify as follows

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{15}{4}\,\pi\,\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\,\frac{1}{\left(\hat{d}_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\;,

(148)

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 15\,\pi\,\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\,\left|J_{2}\right|\,\frac{P^{2}}{\left(\hat{d}_{\sigma}\right)^{4}}\;,

(149)

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$n$}^{{\rm Q}\times{\rm Q}}_{\rm 2PN}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{15}{2}\,\pi\,\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\,\left|J_{2}\right|^{2}\,\frac{P^{4}}{\left(\hat{d}_{\sigma}\right)^{6}}\;.

(150)

These terms are smaller than
1\,{\rm nas}
for grazing light rays at the giant planets, hence they are negligible for astrometry on the sub-micro-arcsecond
level and nano-arcsecond level of accuracy. In contrast, the upper limits for the 1PN terms (
139
) - (
140
) and the
enhanced 2PN terms (
144
) - (
146
) remain unaffected and keep their validity under condition

\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\leq 0
.

For grazing light rays,
\hat{d}_{\sigma}=P_{A}
, the numerical magnitude of the upper limits (
139
) and (
140
)
coincide with (
106
) and (
107
), respectively. Their numerical values were already given in Table
2
.
Similarly, in order to quantify the numerical magnitude of the upper limits (
144
) - (
146
)
we consider a light ray grazing at the surface of the massive body:
\hat{d}_{\sigma}=P_{A}
.
The numerical values of these upper limits for such configurations are presented in Table
4
for the giant planets of the Solar System.

All values are given in micro-arcseconds.

) caused by the monopole structure is considerably larger
(for numerical values see Table

) and

Article_Zschocke1

.

Klioner2003b
;

.

) and (

) caused

) than the upper limits of the 2PN quadrupole terms in
(

) and (

) of the total light deflection (for numerical values see Table
3
).

The
enhanced 2PN terms
of the quadrupole amount up to
0.95\,\hbox{\rm$\mu$as}
and
0.29\,\hbox{\rm$\mu$as}
for grazing rays at Jupiter and Saturn, hence they are still below
the present state-of-the-art in astrometric measurements. For instance, the aimed accuracy of the ESA astrometry mission
Gaia

is about
5\,\hbox{\rm$\mu$as}
in the final data release getting published after the end of mission expected around
2025

Gaia_Archive
.
But in view of the impressive advancements of astrometric science during recent decades it is certain that future ground-based or space-based astrometric
measurements aiming at the sub-micro-arcsecond level will be able to detect the quadrupole light deflection effect in 2PN approximation.

IX
Summary and Outlook

The impressive progress in astrometric science makes it necessary to consider several subtle effects in the
theory of light propagation. In particular, for high precision astrometry on the sub-micro-arcsecond level of accuracy
2PN effects need to be investigated. Thus far, however, only the impact of the monopole structure of massive bodies on
2PN light propagation has been considered in the literature. These 2PN effects of the monopole case have thoroughly been studied
during recent years and in meanwhile a detailed understanding of these 2PN effects has been achieved.

On the other side, actually nothing is known about 2PN light deflection effects beyond the monopole structure of the massive bodies.
For determining the geodesic equation in 2PN approximation one needs the metric of one body at rest in 2PN approximation.
For that, the Multipole Post-Minkowskian (MPM) formalism has been used, which
allows to determine the gravitational field of some massive body being of arbitrary shape, inner structure
and rotational motion. According to the MPM approach, the gravitational field of some massive body is
described by a multipole decomposition in terms of symmetric and trace-free mass-multipoles
\hat{M}_{L}
(shape and inner structure of the body)
and spin-multipoles
\hat{S}_{L}
(rotational motions and inner circulations of the body).

In this investigation we have considered the problem of light propagation in 2PN approximation in the field of one body at rest with
time-independent monopole and quadrupole structure,
\hat{M}_{L}=\{M,\hat{M}_{ab}\}
, while higher mass-multipoles as well as spin-multipoles
of the body have been neglected. The primary results of this investigation are the following:

{\bf 1.}

The coordinate velocity (first integration of geodesic equation)
and the trajectory (second integration of geodesic equation)
of a light signal has been determined in 2PN approximation, which propagates in the gravitational field
of one body at rest, where the monopole structure and the quadrupole structure of the body has been taken into account.
The first integration and the second integration can be reduced in each case to only four master integrals,
Eqs. (
174
) - (
177
) and Eqs. (
193
) - (
196
).
These integrals can be solved in closed form to any order by recurrence relations as given by Eqs. (
179
) - (
182
) and
Eqs. (
198
) - (
201
).

{\bf 2.}

The solutions for the first and second integration of geodesic equation are given by

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}\left(t\right)}{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}+\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 1PN}}{c}+\frac{\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 2PN}}{c}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right),

(151)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 2PN}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right),

(152)

where the 1PN and 2PN corrections
\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 1PN}
and
\Delta\dot{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}_{\rm 2PN}
are given by
Eqs. (
61
) - (
63
) and Eqs. (
82
) - (
85
),
respectively, while the 1PN and 2PN corrections
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}
and
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 2PN}
are given by
Eqs. (
65
) - (
67
)
and Eqs. (
88
) - (
91
), respectively.
These solutions are valid for any astrometric configuration between source, body, and observer, that means for

\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\leq 0
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}>0
as well as
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}\leq 1
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{1}>0
.

{\bf 3.}

Using the given solution of the geodesic equation in 2PN approximation, the total light deflection has been determined in Eq. (
103
).
The upper limits of the 1PN terms of the total light deflection are given by Eqs. (
106
) - (
107
) and their numerical
values are given in Table
2
. The upper limits of the 2PN terms of the total light deflection are given by
Eqs. (
108
) - (
110
). These upper limits are valid for any astrometric configuration between light source, massive body,
and observer. For configurations with
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\leq 0
(body is between source and observer) the upper limits of the 2PN terms of
total light deflection are given by the inequalities (
112
) - (
114
)
and turn out to be less than
1
nano-arcsecond as presented in Table
3
.

{\bf 4.}

In view of the smallness of the 2PN terms in the total light deflection
one might believe that the 2PN quadrupole corrections are negligible even for high-precision astrometry on the sub-micro-arcsecond level of accuracy.
This is, however, not true. It is a well-known fact that there are
enhanced terms
for the case of 2PN light deflection in the gravitational field
of one monopole at rest, which are proportional to
x_{1}/\hat{d}_{\sigma}
and given by the inequality (
144
),

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 16\,\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\,\frac{1}{\left(\hat{d}_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\,\frac{x_{1}}{\hat{d}_{\sigma}}\;,

(153)

where
x_{1}
is the distance between massive body and observer and
\hat{d}_{\sigma}
is the impact vector of the light ray. Hence
x_{1}/\hat{d}_{\sigma}\gg 1
for
grazing rays where the impact vector equals the radius of the body. These
enhanced 2PN terms
amount up to
16
micro-arcseconds for a grazing light ray at
Jupiter and
4
micro-arcseconds for a grazing light ray at Saturn (cf. Table
4
), besides that the total 2PN light deflection
for one monopole at rest is less than
1
nano-arcsecond for grazing light rays at Jupiter
Article_Zschocke1
;
Teyssandier
;
AshbyBertotti2010
.

Our considerations have revealed that there exist similar
enhanced 2PN terms
in the light deflection caused by the quadrupole structure of the body,
which are also proportional to the large factor
x_{1}/\hat{d}_{\sigma}
. The upper limits of these
enhanced 2PN terms
are given by the inequalities
(
145
) - (
146
),

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm Q}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 64\,\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\,\left|J_{2}\right|\,\frac{P^{2}}{\left(\hat{d}_{\sigma}\right)^{4}}\,\frac{x_{1}}{\hat{d}_{\sigma}}\;,

(154)

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$n$}_{\rm 2PN}^{{\rm Q}\times{\rm Q}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 48\,\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\,\left|J_{2}\right|^{2}\,\frac{P^{4}}{\left(\hat{d}_{\sigma}\right)^{6}}\,\frac{x_{1}}{\hat{d}_{\sigma}}\;.

(155)

The enhanced 2PN quadrupole terms in (
154
) amount up to
0.95
micro-arcseconds for a grazing light ray at Jupiter and
0.29

micro-arcseconds for a grazing light ray at Saturn (cf. Table
4
), which shows that they are relevant on the sub-micro-arcsecond level of astrometric
accuracy. It has been emphasized that these
enhanced 2PN terms
in (
144
) - (
146
) are
solely caused by the fact that the light deflection at observer’s position in (
118
) is expressed in terms of the impact vector
given by Eq. (
130
) which is indispensable for modeling of real astrometric measurements.

Finally, it is noticed that a set of several checks has been performed: (a) the calculations which lead to the time-independent coefficients
(
256
) - (
280
) and (
) - (
302
) have been performed twice, namely
analytically and by means of the computer algebra system
Maple

Maple
.
(b) it is a non-trivial fact that their prefactors are either integers or half-integral numbers.
(c) it has been checked analytically that the tangent vector
\nu
in (
95
) is a unit vector.
(d) it has been checked numerically by
Maple
that the null-condition (
20
) is satisfied along the entire light ray.
(e) our results in case of 1PN and 2PN monopole as well as in case of 1PN quadrupole agree with known achievements in the literature.

Actually, one has to realize that at the sub-micro-arcsecond level of accuracy celestial light sources like stars and quasars, cannot be treated as pointlike
but extended objects. Accordingly, one may separate the surface of celestial objects into sufficiently small surface elements such, that each individual surface
element can then be treated as a pointlike light source. This treatment becomes more relevant the higher the aimed astrometric accuracies are.

Furthermore, in the limit of geometrical optics, light propagates along null-geodesics, governed by the geodesic equation (
1
)
and the null-condition (
2
). In reality, however, light propagates as electromagnetic wave, and one has to investigate at which scale of
accuracy the wave nature of light must be taken into account. Such an investigation has been performed in
Geometrical_Optics
, where is was found that
deviations from geometric optics are negligible even on the nano-arcsecond level, because the wavelength of electromagnetic waves in the optical band is much
smaller than the curvature of space-time in the Solar System. In this respect we also refer to the recent investigations
Turyshev1
;
Turyshev2
;
Turyshev3

where by using the eikonal approach the issue of image formation has been considered, which could be observed with telescopes located far out of the solar system.

The investigations presented are a basis for further considerations in the area of high-precision astrometry.
In particular, in a subsequent investigation the boundary value problem of geodesic equation will be solved in 2PN approximation for the case of one body
at rest with monopole and quadrupole structure, where also the Shapiro time delay will comprehensively be treated.
Furthermore, the approach presented is also applicable to solve the problem of 2PN light propagation in the gravitational field of massive bodies with
higher multipoles beyond the monopole and quadrupole structure of the bodies.

Appendix A
Notation

Throughout the investigation the following notation is in use:

•

G
is the Newtonian constant of gravitation.

•

c
is the vacuum speed of light in Minkowskian space-time.

•

M
is the rest mass of the body defined by Eq. (
17
).

•

\hat{M}_{ab}
is the quadrupole moment of the body defined by Eq. (
18
).

•

m=G\,M/c^{2}
is the Schwarzschild radius of the body.

•

P
denotes the equatorial radius of the body.

•

Lower case Latin indices
i
,
j
, …take values
1,2,3
.

•

\dot{f}
denotes total time derivative of
f
.

•

f_{\,,\,i}=\partial f/\partial x^{i}
denotes partial derivative of
f
with respect to
x^{i}
.

•

\delta_{ij}=\delta^{ij}={\rm diag}\left(+1,+1,+1\right)
is Kronecker delta.

•

n!=n\left(n-1\right)\left(n-2\right)\cdot\cdot\cdot 2\cdot 1
is the factorial for positive integer
\left(0!=1\right)
.

•

n!!=n\left(n-2\right)\left(n-4\right)\cdot\cdot\cdot\left(2\;{\rm or}\;1\right)
is the double factorial for positive integer
\left(0!!=1\right)
.

•

\varepsilon_{ijk}=\varepsilon^{ijk}
with
\varepsilon_{123}=+1
is the fully anti-symmetric Levi-Civita symbol.

•

Triplet of three-vectors are in boldface: e.g.
a
,
b
,
\sigma
,
x
.

•

Contravariant components of three-vectors:
a^{i}=\left(a^{\,1},a^{2},a^{3}\right)
.

•

Absolute value of a three-vector:
a=|\mbox{\boldmath$a$}|=\sqrt{a^{\,1}\,a^{\,1}+a^{2}\,a^{2}+a^{3}\,a^{3}}
.

•

Scalar product of three-vectors:
\mbox{\boldmath$a$}\,\cdot\,\mbox{\boldmath$b$}=\delta_{ij}\,a^{i}\,b^{j}
.

•

Vector product of two three-vectors:
\left(\mbox{\boldmath$a$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$b$}\right)^{i}=\varepsilon_{ijk}\,a^{j}\,b^{k}
.

•

Angle between three-vectors
a
and
b
is denoted by
\delta\left(\mbox{\boldmath$a$},\mbox{\boldmath$b$}\right)
.

•

Lower case Greek indices take values 0,1,2,3.

•

f_{\,,\,\mu}=\partial f/\partial x^{\mu}
denotes partial derivative of
f
with respect to
x^{\mu}
.

•

\eta_{\alpha\beta}=\eta^{\alpha\beta}={\rm diag}\left(-1,+1,+1,+1\right)
is the metric tensor of flat space-time.

•

g_{\alpha\beta}
and
g^{\alpha\beta}
are the covariant and contravariant components of the metric tensor.

•

Contravariant components of four-vectors:
a^{\mu}=\left(a^{\,0},a^{\,1},a^{2},a^{3}\right)
.

•

milli-arcsecond (mas):
1\,{\rm mas}\simeq 4.85\times 10^{-9}\,{\rm rad}
.

•

micro-arcsecond (
\mu
as
):
1\,\hbox{\rm$\mu$as}\simeq 4.85\times 10^{-12}\,{\rm rad}
.

•

nano-arcsecond (nas):
1\,{\rm nas}\simeq 4.85\times 10^{-15}\,{\rm rad}
.

•

{\rm au}=1.496\times 10^{11}\,{\rm m}
is the astronomical unit

•

repeated indices are implicitly summed over (
Einstein’s
sum convention).

Appendix B
The STF tensors
\hat{n}_{L}

In this appendix we summarize some formulae of symmetric trace-free (STF) tensors which are relevant for our investigation.
Further standard STF notation and some STF relations can be found in
Thorne
;
Blanchet_Damour1
;
Coope1
;
Coope2
;
Coope3
;
Soffel_Hartmann
;
see also appendix B in
Zschocke_2PM_Metric
. The symmetric part of some tensor
{\cal J}^{L}
with indices
L=i_{1},\dots i_{l}
is given by

\displaystyle{\cal J}^{\left(L\right)}={\cal J}^{\left(i_{1}\dots i_{l}\right)}=\frac{1}{l!}\sum\limits_{\sigma}{\cal J}^{i_{\sigma\left(1\right)}\dots i_{\sigma\left(l\right)}}\;,

(156)

where
\sigma
is running over all permutations of the indices
1,\dots,l
. If one subtracts all traces from (
156
) then
one gets the symmetric trace-free (STF) part of tensor
{\cal J}^{L}
. A general formula for the STF part of some tensor is provided by Eq. (2.2) in
Thorne

or Eqs. (A 2a) - (A 2c) in
Blanchet_Damour1
. Here, we will not repeat that formula, but just apply for a specific case of it,
as given by Eq. (
161
) below.

In particular, in our investigation the following Cartesian tensors are of primary importance,

\displaystyle x^{L}=x^{i_{1}}\,\dots\,x^{i_{l}}\quad{\rm and}\quad n^{L}=\frac{x^{i_{1}}}{x}\,\dots\,\frac{x^{i_{l}}}{x}

(157)

where
L=i_{1},\dots,i_{l}
is a multi-index,
x^{i}
are the spatial coordinates of some arbitrary field point in space and
x=\left|\mbox{\boldmath$x$}\right|
.
Note that
x_{L}=x^{L}
and
n_{L}=n^{L}
.
For instance,

\displaystyle n^{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{x^{a}x^{b}}{x^{2}}\;,

(158)

\displaystyle n^{abcd}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{x^{a}x^{b}x^{c}x^{d}}{x^{4}}\;.

(159)

The symmetric trace-free (STF) part of these Cartesian tensors in (
157
) are denoted by

\displaystyle\hat{x}^{L}={\rm STF}_{i_{1}\dots i_{l}}\;x^{i_{1}}\,\dots\,x^{i_{l}}\;{\rm and}\;\hat{n}^{L}={\rm STF}_{i_{1}\dots i_{l}}\;\frac{x^{i_{1}}}{x}\,\dots\,\frac{x^{i_{l}}}{x}

(160)

where
{\rm STF}_{i_{1}\dots i_{l}}
is the symmetrization and trace-free operation with respect to the spatial indices
i_{1}\dots i_{l}
.
Note that
\hat{x}_{L}=\hat{x}^{L}
and
\hat{n}_{L}=\hat{n}^{L}
.
A general formula to express
\hat{n}^{L}
in terms of
n^{L}
is provided by (cf. Eq. (1.154) in
Poisson_Will
)

\displaystyle\hat{n}^{L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=0}^{[l/2]}\left(-1\right)^{k}\frac{\left(2l-2k-1\right)!!}{\left(2l-1\right)!!}\;\frac{l!}{\left(l-2k\right)!\left(2k\right)!!}\;\delta^{(i_{1}i_{2}}\dots\delta^{i_{2k-1}i_{2k}}\;n^{i_{2k+1\dots i_{l}})}

(161)

where round brackets of the tensorial term indicate symmetrization according to Eq. (
156
), double factorial is denoted by
m!!

and
[l/2]=l/2
for even
l
, while
[l/2]=(l-1)/2
for odd
l
.
For instance,

\displaystyle\hat{n}^{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{ab}-\frac{1}{3}\,\delta^{ab}\;,

(162)

\displaystyle\hat{n}^{abcd}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{abcd}-\frac{1}{7}\left(\delta^{ab}n^{cd}+\delta^{ac}n^{bd}+\delta^{ad}n^{bc}+\delta^{bc}n^{ad}+\delta^{bd}n^{ac}+\delta^{cd}n^{ab}\right)+\frac{1}{35}\left(\delta^{ab}\,\delta^{cd}+\delta^{ac}\,\delta^{bd}+\delta^{ad}\,\delta^{bc}\right)\,,

(163)

\displaystyle\hat{n}^{abcde\!f}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{abcde\!f}-\frac{1}{11}\left(\delta^{ab}\,n^{cde\!f}+\emph{sym.}\right)+\frac{1}{99}\left(\delta^{ab}\,\delta^{cd}\,n^{e\!f}+\emph{sym.}\right)-\frac{1}{693}\left(\delta^{ab}\,\delta^{cd}\,\delta^{e\!f}+\emph{sym.}\right).

(164)

These STF tensors appear in the 2PN metric coefficients in Eqs. (
69
) - (
70
).
The expressions (
162
) and (
163
) were also given by Eqs. (1.153a) and (1.153c) in
Poisson_Will
;
see also Eqs. (1.8.2) and (1.8.4) in
Poisson_Lecture_Notes
; the abbreviation
sym.
in (
164
)
means symmetrization with respect to the indices.

The geodesic equation in 1PN and 2PN approximation, Eqs. (
44
) and (
71
), contains spatial derivatives
of the metric coefficients in (
69
) - (
70
).
Therefore, one needs the spatial derivative of the STF tensor in (
161
).
The general formula is given by (cf. Eq. (A 24) in
Blanchet_Damour1
)

\displaystyle\left(\hat{n}^{L}\right)_{,\,k}=\frac{l+1}{x}\,n^{k}\,\hat{n}^{L}-\frac{2l+1}{x}\,\hat{n}^{kL}\;,

(165)

which can finally be expressed in terms of
n^{L}
by means of relation (
161
).

As explained in the text below Eq. (
71
), that field point becomes the spatial coordinate of the light ray, which is expanded in terms
of the unperturbed light ray; see Eqs. (
227
) - (
229
) in Appendix
E
.
Therefore, one finally needs to consider the tensor

n_{L}
in (
157
) and the STF tensor
\hat{n}_{L}
in (
160
) where
the field point is replaced by the unperturbed light ray, that means

\displaystyle n^{L}_{\rm N}=\frac{x_{\rm N}^{i_{1}}}{x_{\rm N}}\,\dots\,\frac{x_{\rm N}^{i_{l}}}{x_{\rm N}}\;,

(166)

and

\displaystyle\hat{n}^{L}_{\rm N}={\rm STF}_{i_{1}\dots i_{l}}\;\frac{x_{\rm N}^{i_{1}}}{x_{\rm N}}\,\dots\,\frac{x_{\rm N}^{i_{l}}}{x_{\rm N}}\;,

(167)

where
x^{i}_{\rm N}
are the spatial coordinates of the unperturbed light ray as given by Eq. (
31
)
and
x_{\rm N}=\left|\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right|
.
From (
161
) it is obvious that the general formula to express
\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{L}
in terms of
n_{\rm N}^{L}
reads

\displaystyle\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=0}^{[l/2]}\left(-1\right)^{k}\frac{\left(2l-2k-1\right)!!}{\left(2l-1\right)!!}\;\frac{l!}{\left(l-2k\right)!\left(2k\right)!!}\;\delta^{(i_{1}i_{2}}\dots\delta^{i_{2k-1}i_{2k}}\;n_{\rm N}^{i_{2k+1\dots i_{l}})}\;.

(168)

Similarly, we need the spatial derivative of
\hat{n}^{L}
in (
165
),
where (after derivation) the field point
x
is replaced by the unperturbed light ray
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}
, that means

\displaystyle\left(\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{L}\right)_{,\,k}=\frac{l+1}{x}\,n_{\rm N}^{k}\,\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{L}-\frac{2l+1}{x}\,\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{kL}\;.

(169)

By means of (
168
) one may express such quantities in terms of
n_{\rm N}^{L}
.
In what follows, such terms are slightly imprecise called
spatial derivative of
\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{L}
, while these terms are actually spatial derivatives of

\hat{n}^{L}
where after the differentiation the field point is replaced by the unperturbed light ray.

Appendix C
The auxiliary tensors
T^{ijab}_{\rm N}
and
T^{iijabcd}_{\rm N}

These tensors
T^{ijab}_{\rm N}
and
T^{iijabcd}_{\rm N}
are composed of STF tensors
\hat{n}^{L}_{\rm N}
, nevertheless they themselves
are neither symmetric in their indices nor trace-free. They are just abbreviations to simplify the notations.
The auxiliary tensor
T^{ijab}_{\rm N}
is defined by

\displaystyle T_{\rm N}^{ijab}=\frac{15}{2}\,\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{ijab}+\frac{32}{7}\,\delta^{ij}\,\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{ab}-\frac{6}{7}\,\delta^{ai}\,\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{jb}-\frac{6}{7}\,\delta^{aj}\,\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{ib}\;,

(170)

where the STF tensors
\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{ab}
and
\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{abcd}
were given by Eq. (
168
).
The spatial derivative of
T^{ijab}_{\rm N}
reads

\displaystyle\left(T_{\rm N}^{ijab}\right)_{,\,k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{15}{2}\left(\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{ijab}\right)_{,\,k}+\frac{32}{7}\,\delta^{ij}\left(\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{ab}\right)_{,\,k}-\frac{6}{7}\,\delta^{ai}\left(\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{jb}\right)_{,\,k}-\frac{6}{7}\,\delta^{aj}\left(\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{ib}\right)_{,\,k}\;,

(171)

where the terms on the r.h.s. are spatial derivatives of STF tensors and were given by Eq.(
169
).
The auxiliary tensor
T^{ijabcd}_{\rm N}
is defined by

\displaystyle T_{\rm N}^{ijabcd}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{75}{4}\,\hat{n}^{ijabcd}_{\rm N}-\frac{90}{11}\,\delta^{bd}\,\hat{n}^{ijac}_{\rm N}+\frac{27}{11}\,\delta^{ij}\,\hat{n}^{abcd}_{\rm N}-\frac{25}{84}\,\delta^{ac}\,\delta^{bd}\,\hat{n}^{ij}_{\rm N}+\frac{83}{42}\,\delta^{bc}\,\delta^{ij}\,\hat{n}^{ad}_{\rm N}+\frac{9}{11}\,\delta^{jb}\,\hat{n}^{acdi}_{\rm N}

(172)

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt+\frac{9}{11}\,\delta^{ib}\,\hat{n}^{acdj}_{\rm N}-\frac{5}{42}\,\delta^{jc}\,\delta^{bd}\,\hat{n}^{ai}_{\rm N}-\frac{5}{42}\,\delta^{ic}\,\delta^{bd}\,\hat{n}^{aj}_{\rm N}+\frac{10}{21}\,\delta^{ci}\,\delta^{dj}\,\hat{n}^{ab}_{\rm N}-\frac{23}{84}\,\delta^{bi}\,\delta^{jc}\,\hat{n}^{ad}_{\rm N}-\frac{23}{84}\,\delta^{bj}\,\delta^{ic}\,\hat{n}^{ad}_{\rm N}\;,

where the STF tensors
\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{ab}
,
\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{abcd}
, and
\hat{n}_{\rm N}^{abcde\!f}
were given
by Eq. (
168
).
The spatial derivative of
T^{ijabcd}_{\rm N}
reads

\displaystyle\left(T_{\rm N}^{ijabcd}\right)_{,\,k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{75}{4}\left(\hat{n}^{ijabcd}_{\rm N}\right)_{,\,k}-\frac{90}{11}\,\delta^{bd}\left(\hat{n}^{ijac}_{\rm N}\right)_{,\,k}+\frac{27}{11}\,\delta^{ij}\left(\hat{n}^{abcd}_{\rm N}\right)_{,\,k}-\frac{25}{84}\,\delta^{ac}\,\delta^{bd}\left(\hat{n}^{ij}_{\rm N}\right)_{,\,k}

(173)

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt+\frac{83}{42}\,\delta^{bc}\,\delta^{ij}\left(\hat{n}^{ad}_{\rm N}\right)_{,\,k}+\frac{9}{11}\,\delta^{jb}\left(\hat{n}^{acdi}_{\rm N}\right)_{,\,k}+\frac{9}{11}\,\delta^{ib}\left(\hat{n}^{acdj}_{\rm N}\right)_{,\,k}-\frac{5}{42}\,\delta^{jc}\,\delta^{bd}\left(\hat{n}^{ai}_{\rm N}\right)_{,\,k}

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt-\frac{5}{42}\,\delta^{ic}\,\delta^{bd}\left(\hat{n}^{aj}_{\rm N}\right)_{,\,k}+\frac{10}{21}\,\delta^{ci}\,\delta^{dj}\left(\hat{n}^{ab}_{\rm N}\right)_{,\,k}-\frac{23}{84}\,\delta^{bi}\,\delta^{jc}\left(\hat{n}^{ad}_{\rm N}\right)_{,\,k}-\frac{23}{84}\,\delta^{bj}\,\delta^{ic}\left(\hat{n}^{ad}_{\rm N}\right)_{,\,k}\;,

where the terms on the r.h.s. are spatial derivatives of STF tensors and were given by Eq. (
169
).

Appendix D
Scalar master integrals

D.1
Scalar master integrals for the first integration

The first integration of geodesic equation in 1PN and 2PN approximation can be reduced to finally only four kind of scalar master integrals,

\displaystyle\dot{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{-\infty}^{t}dc{\rm t}\;\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{n}}\;,

(174)

\displaystyle\dot{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{-\infty}^{t}dc{\rm t}\;\frac{1}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{n}}\;,

(175)

\displaystyle\dot{\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{-\infty}^{t}dc{\rm t}\;\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{n}}\;\ln\frac{x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{0}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}\;,

(176)

\displaystyle\dot{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{-\infty}^{t}dc{\rm t}\;\frac{1}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{n}}\;\ln\frac{x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{0}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}\;,

(177)

where
\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}=\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\left({\rm t}\right)
and
x_{\rm N}=x_{\rm N}\left({\rm t}\right)
are functions of the
integration variable
{\rm t}
according to (
31
)
and (
32
).
Clearly, the limits

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow-\infty}\dot{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;,\quad\lim_{t\rightarrow-\infty}\dot{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)=0\;,\quad\lim_{t\rightarrow-\infty}\dot{\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)=0\;,\quad\lim_{t\rightarrow-\infty}\dot{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)=0\;,

(178)

are in accordance with relation (
26
).
The scalar master integrals for the first integration can be given in closed form,

\displaystyle\dot{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{n-2}\,\frac{1}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{n-2}}\;,

(179)

\displaystyle\dot{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n-2}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\left(n-3\right)\dot{\cal X}_{\left(n-2\right)}\left(t\right)+\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{n-2}}\right),

(180)

\displaystyle\dot{\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{n-2}\left(\dot{\cal X}_{\left(n-1\right)}\left(t\right)+\frac{1}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{n-2}}\,\ln\frac{x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{0}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}\right),

(181)

\displaystyle\dot{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n-2}\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\!\Bigg(\!\!\left(n-3\right)\!\dot{\cal Z}_{\left(n-2\right)}\left(t\right)-\frac{1}{n-3}\frac{1}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{n-3}}+\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{n-2}}\ln\frac{x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{0}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}\!\Bigg),

(182)

where
n\geq 3
in Eqs. (
179
) and (
181
) and

n\geq 4
in Eqs. (
180
) and (
182
). While (
179
) represents the
explicit solution of the integrals (
174
) to any order, the implicit
recurrence relations (
180
) - (
182
) allow us to determine the explicit solutions of
the integrals (
175
) - (
177
) to any order of the inverse powers in
n
as soon as the first orders of them are known,
which have to be determined directly by using standard integrals. Furthermore, they are very useful for estimating their upper limits of impact on
light deflection and they can be used to make some cross checks of the explicit solutions for the integrals. These integrals
(
179
) - (
182
) are stable, that means they are convergent with respect to the limit
n\rightarrow\infty
.
The explicit solutions for the first few orders of the inverse powers in
n
are given by

\displaystyle\dot{\cal X}_{\left(2\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d_{\sigma}}\left(\arctan\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{d_{\sigma}}+\frac{\pi}{2}\right),

(183)

\displaystyle\dot{\cal X}_{\left(3\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{\rm N}}+1\right),

(184)

\displaystyle\dot{\cal X}_{\left(4\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{2}}+\frac{1}{d_{\sigma}}\left(\arctan\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{d_{\sigma}}+\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\right),

(185)

\displaystyle\dot{\cal X}_{\left(5\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{\rm N}}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{3}}+\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\right),

(186)

\displaystyle\dot{\cal X}_{\left(6\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{4}}+\frac{3}{2}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{2}}+\frac{3}{2}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}\left(\arctan\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{d_{\sigma}}+\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\right),

(187)

\displaystyle\dot{\cal X}_{\left(7\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{8}{15}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{\rm N}}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{4}}+\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{3}}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}+\frac{3}{8}\,\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{5}}+\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{4}}\right),

(188)

\displaystyle\dot{\cal Y}_{\left(5\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{3}\,\frac{1}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{3}}\,\ln\frac{x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{0}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}-\frac{1}{6}\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{2}}+\frac{1}{d_{\sigma}}\left(\arctan\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{d_{\sigma}}+\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\right),

(189)

\displaystyle\dot{\cal Z}_{\left(3\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{\rm N}}+1\right)\ln\frac{x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{0}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}-\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\ln\frac{x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{2\,x_{\rm N}}\right),

(190)

\displaystyle\dot{\cal Z}_{\left(5\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\!\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{\rm N}}\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{3}}+\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\!\right)\ln\frac{x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{0}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}-\frac{1}{6}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\frac{1}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{2}}-\frac{2}{3}\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{4}}\left(\ln\frac{x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{2\,x_{\rm N}}\right),

where the time arguments have been omitted in the terms on the r.h.s. in order to simplify the notation.

For the expression of the total light deflection one needs the limits in (
101
).
Using
\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}/x_{\rm N}=1
, which follows from (
31
)
and (
32
), one obtains:

\displaystyle\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(2\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{d_{\sigma}}\;,\;\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(3\right)}=\frac{2}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\;,\;\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(4\right)}=\frac{\pi}{2}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}\;,\;\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(5\right)}=\frac{4}{3}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{4}}\;,\;\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(6\right)}=\frac{3}{8}\,\frac{\pi}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{5}}\;,\;\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(7\right)}=\frac{16}{15}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{6}}\;,\;

\displaystyle\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(8\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{5}{16}\,\frac{\pi}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{7}}\;,\;\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(9\right)}=\frac{32}{35}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{8}}\;,\;\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(10\right)}=\frac{35}{128}\,\frac{\pi}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{9}}\;,\;\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(11\right)}=\frac{256}{315}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{10}}\;,\;\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(12\right)}=\frac{63}{256}\,\frac{\pi}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{11}}\;,\;

\displaystyle\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(13\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{512}{693}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{12}}\;,\;\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(14\right)}=\frac{231}{1024}\,\frac{\pi}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{13}}\;,\;\dot{\cal Y}^{\infty}_{\left(5\right)}=-\frac{1}{6}\,\frac{\pi}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}\;,\;\dot{\cal Y}^{\infty}_{\left(7\right)}=-\frac{3}{40}\,\frac{\pi}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{5}}\;,\;\dot{\cal Y}^{\infty}_{\left(9\right)}=-\frac{5}{112}\,\frac{\pi}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{7}}\;.

(192)

D.2
Scalar master integrals for the second integration

The second integration of geodesic equation in 1PN and 2PN approximation can be reduced to finally only four scalar master integrals,

\displaystyle{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{t_{0}}^{t}dc{\rm t}\;\dot{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left({\rm t}\right)={\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)-{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t_{0}\right),

(193)

\displaystyle{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{t_{0}}^{t}dc{\rm t}\;\dot{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left({\rm t}\right)={\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)-{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t_{0}\right),

(194)

\displaystyle{\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{t_{0}}^{t}dc{\rm t}\;\dot{\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}\left({\rm t}\right)={\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)-{\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t_{0}\right),

(195)

\displaystyle{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{t_{0}}^{t}dc{\rm t}\;\dot{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}\left({\rm t}\right)={\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)-{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t_{0}\right).

(196)

Clearly, the limits

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;,\quad\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)=0\;,\quad\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)=0\;,\quad\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t,t_{0}\right)=0\;,

(197)

are in accordance with relation (
29
).
The scalar master integrals for the second integration can be given in closed form,

\displaystyle{\cal W}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{n-2}\,\dot{\cal X}_{\left(n-2\right)}\left(t\right),

(198)

\displaystyle{\cal X}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n-2}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\dot{\cal W}_{\left(n-2\right)}\left(t\right)+\left(n-3\right){\cal X}_{\left(n-2\right)}\left(t\right)\right),

(199)

\displaystyle{\cal Y}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{n-2}\left(\dot{\cal Z}_{\left(n-2\right)}\left(t\right)+{\cal X}_{\left(n-1\right)}\left(t\right)\right),

(200)

\displaystyle{\cal Z}_{\left(n\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n-2}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\Bigg(\left(n-3\right){\cal Z}_{\left(n-2\right)}\left(t\right)-\frac{1}{n-3}\dot{\cal X}_{\left(n-3\right)}\left(t\right)+\dot{\cal Y}_{\left(n-2\right)}\left(t\right)\Bigg),

(201)

where
n\geq 4
in Eq. (
198
) and

n\geq 5
in Eqs. (
199
) - (
201
).
The recurrence relations (
198
) - (
201
) allow us to determine the solutions of
the integrals (
193
) - (
196
) to any order of the power
n
as soon as the very few orders of them are known,
which have to be determined directly by using standard integrals.
Furthermore, they are very useful for estimating
their upper limits of impact on time delay and light deflection and they can be used to cross check some of the explicit solutions for the integrals.
These integrals (
198
) - (
201
) are stable, that means they are convergent in the limit
n\rightarrow\infty
.
The explicit solutions for the first few orders of the power in
n

are given by (constants which cancel each other due to (
193
) - (
196
) have been omitted)

\displaystyle{\cal W}_{\left(3\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln\left(x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right),

(202)

\displaystyle{\cal W}_{\left(4\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{d_{\sigma}}\,\arctan\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{d_{\sigma}}\;,

(203)

\displaystyle{\cal W}_{\left(5\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{3}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\,\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{\rm N}}\;,

(204)

\displaystyle{\cal W}_{\left(6\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{2}}+\frac{1}{d_{\sigma}}\,\arctan\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{d_{\sigma}}\right),

(205)

\displaystyle{\cal W}_{\left(7\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{15}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{\rm N}}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{3}}\right),

(206)

\displaystyle{\cal X}_{\left(2\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{d_{\sigma}}\,\left(\arctan\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{d_{\sigma}}+\frac{\pi}{2}\right)-\ln x_{\rm N}\;,

(207)

\displaystyle{\cal X}_{\left(3\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(x_{\rm N}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right),

(208)

\displaystyle{\cal X}_{\left(4\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}\left(\arctan\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{d_{\sigma}}+\frac{\pi}{2}\right),

(209)

\displaystyle{\cal X}_{\left(5\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{3}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{x_{\rm N}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{x_{\rm N}}\right),

(210)

\displaystyle{\cal X}_{\left(6\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{2}}-3\,\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}\left(\arctan\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{d_{\sigma}}+\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\right),

(211)

\displaystyle{\cal X}_{\left(7\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\frac{8}{15}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{x_{\rm N}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{4}}-\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{x_{\rm N}}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}-\frac{1}{8}\,\frac{1}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{3}}+\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{4}}\right),

(212)

\displaystyle{\cal Y}_{\left(5\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{3}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\left(\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{\rm N}}+1\right)\ln\frac{x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{0}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{d_{\sigma}}\left(\arctan\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{d_{\sigma}}+\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\right)+\,\frac{1}{3}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\,\ln\frac{x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{2\,x_{\rm N}}\;,

\displaystyle{\cal Z}_{\left(3\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{x_{\rm N}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\,\ln\frac{x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{0}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}+\frac{1}{d_{\sigma}}\,\arctan\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{d_{\sigma}}-\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\,\ln\frac{x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{2\,x_{\rm N}}\;,

(214)

\displaystyle{\cal Z}_{\left(5\right)}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{x_{\rm N}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}+\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{x_{\rm N}}\right)\ln\frac{x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{x_{0}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}+\frac{1}{6}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}\,\arctan\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{d_{\sigma}}-\frac{2}{3}\,\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{4}}\,\ln\frac{x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}}{2\,x_{\rm N}}\;,

where the time arguments have been omitted in order to simplify the notation. Note that terms like

\ln\left(x_{\rm N}-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)
in (
202
) and

\ln x_{\rm N}
in (
207
) are well-defined in view of (
193
) and (
194
).

Appendix E
The terms of 2PN geodesic equation

In this appendix each term of the 2PN geodesic equation (
72
) is given, which can be
written in the following form:

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}^{i}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}_{A}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{B}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{C}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{D}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{E}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{F}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{G}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{H}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{I}^{i}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\ddot{x}_{J}^{i}}{c^{2}}+{\cal O}\left(c^{-6}\right),

(216)

where the individual terms are

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}_{A}^{i}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{00,i}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\right)\;,

(217)

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}_{B}^{i}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\,h_{00,j}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\right)\,\sigma^{i}\sigma^{j}\;,

(218)

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}_{C}^{i}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{00,i}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)\,\frac{\sigma^{j}\Delta\dot{x}^{j}_{\rm 1PN}}{c}\;,

(219)

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}_{D}^{i}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\,h_{00,j}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)\,\frac{\Delta\dot{x}^{j}_{\rm 1PN}}{c}\,\sigma^{i}\;,

(220)

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}_{E}^{i}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\,h_{00,j}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)\,\frac{\Delta\dot{x}^{i}_{\rm 1PN}}{c}\,\sigma^{j}\;,

(221)

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}_{F}^{i}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-h_{00}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)\,h_{00,i}^{(2)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right),

(222)

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}_{G}^{i}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,h_{00,i}^{(4)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right),

(223)

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}_{H}^{i}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-h_{00,j}^{(4)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)\,\sigma^{i}\sigma^{j}\;,

(224)

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}_{I}^{i}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-h_{ij,k}^{(4)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)\,\sigma^{j}\sigma^{k}\;,

(225)

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{x}_{J}^{i}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,h_{jk,i}^{(4)}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\right)\,\sigma^{j}\sigma^{k}\;.

(226)

The following subsections are devoted to each of these
10
individual terms. In what follows, one needs the spatial coordinates
and the inverse powers of their absolute values in 1PN approximation, that is, up to terms of the order
{\cal O}\left(c^{-4}\right)
:

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}+\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}\;,

(227)

\displaystyle x_{\rm 1PN}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{\rm N}+\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\cdot\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}}{x_{\rm N}}\;,

(228)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left(x_{\rm 1PN}\right)^{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{n}}-\frac{n}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{n}}\,\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm N}\cdot\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}}{\left(x_{\rm N}\right)^{2}}\;,

(229)

where Eq. (

) was given by Eq. (

) and expression
\Delta\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{\rm 1PN}
is given
by Eqs. (

Appendix F
Estimation of the expression in (
107
)

In this appendix we want to proof the inequality in (
107
) as instructive example.
That means we consider the upper limit of the absolute value
\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}_{\rm 1PN}^{\rm Q}\big|

where
\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}_{\rm 1PN}^{\rm Q}
is given by Eq. (
97
).
Using
\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}_{\rm 1PN}^{\rm Q}\right)^{k}=\epsilon^{ijk}\sigma^{i}\nu_{\rm 1PN}^{j\,{\rm Q}}
one gets

\displaystyle\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}_{\rm 1PN}^{\rm Q}\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{G\hat{M}_{ab}}{c^{2}}\epsilon^{ijk}\sigma^{i}\left[\sum\limits_{n=5,7}{\cal D}^{j\,ab}_{\left(n\right)}\;\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(n\right)}\right],

(303)

where the time-independent coefficients
D^{j\,ab}_{\left(n\right)}
are given by
Eqs. (
56
) and (
57
).
By inserting the limits (
192
) into (
303
) one obtains

\displaystyle\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{3}\,\frac{G\hat{M}_{ab}}{c^{2}}\,\frac{\epsilon^{ijk}\sigma^{i}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{4}}\left[{\cal D}^{j\,ab}_{\left(5\right)}+\frac{4}{5}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\,{\cal D}^{j\,ab}_{\left(7\right)}\right].

(304)

The origin of the spatial coordinates
x^{1},x^{2},x^{3}
is located at the center of mass of the body.
As stated in Section
VII
, we assume the
x^{3}
-axis of coordinate system to be aligned with
the symmetry axis
\mbox{\boldmath$e$}_{3}
of the massive body. Hence, the quadrupole tensor (
105
) takes the form

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{ab}=M\,J_{2}\,P^{2}\left(\frac{1}{3}\,\delta_{ab}-\delta_{a3}\,\delta_{b3}\right).

(305)

By inserting (
305
) into (
304
) and taking into account that

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}\right|=\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\right)\right|\,,

(306)

which is valid since
\sigma
is a unit-vector, and using

\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\left(\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\right)=\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}
and

\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\left(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\right)=\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}
, one obtains

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\,\frac{GM}{c^{2}}\left|J_{2}\right|\frac{P^{2}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}\left|F_{1}\,\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$d_{\sigma}$}}{d_{\sigma}}+F_{2}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\left(\mbox{\boldmath$e$}_{3}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\right)\right)\right|,

(307)

where the functions read

\displaystyle F_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$e$}_{3}\right)^{2}-4\,\frac{\left(\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$e$}_{3}\right)^{2}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\;,

(308)

\displaystyle F_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\,\frac{\left(\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$e$}_{3}\right)}{d_{\sigma}}\;.

(309)

For determining the limit (
307
) it is necessary to take into account that
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma}=0

which restricts the possible values for the angles
\delta\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$},\mbox{\boldmath$e$}_{3}\right)
and
\delta\left(\mbox{\boldmath$d$}_{\sigma},\mbox{\boldmath$e$}_{3}\right)

(see also endnote
\left[99\right]
). One obtains for the upper limit

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{\rm Q}_{\rm 1PN}\right|\leq 4\frac{GM}{c^{2}}\left|J_{2}\right|\frac{P^{2}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}\;,

(310)

which has also been proven in our article
Zschocke6
(cf. Eq. (12) ibid.). Thus, the validity of relation (
107
) has been shown.

Appendix G
Estimation of the expression in (
108
)

In this appendix we want to proof the inequality in (
108
).
That means we consider the upper limit of the absolute value
\big|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}_{\rm 2PN}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}\big|

where
\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}_{\rm 2PN}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}
is given by Eq. (
98
).
Using
\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}_{\rm 2PN}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}\right)^{k}=\epsilon^{ijk}\sigma^{i}\nu_{\rm 2PN}^{j\,{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}
one gets

\displaystyle\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\,\epsilon^{ijk}\sigma^{i}\left[\sum\limits_{n=2}^{6}{\cal F}^{j}_{\left(n\right)}\;\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(n\right)}+{\cal G}^{j}_{\left(5\right)}\;\dot{\cal Y}^{\infty}_{\left(5\right)}\right],

(311)

where the time-independent coefficients
{\cal F}^{j}_{\left(n\right)}
and
{\cal G}^{j}_{\left(5\right)}
are given by
Eqs. (
247
) - (
251
) and (
252
), respectively.
By inserting the limits (
192
) into (
311
) one obtains

\displaystyle\left[\sum\limits_{n=2}^{6}{\cal F}^{j}_{\left(n\right)}\;\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(n\right)}+{\cal G}^{j}_{\left(5\right)}\;\dot{\cal Y}^{\infty}_{\left(5\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\,\frac{{\cal F}^{j}_{\left(3\right)}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}+\frac{4}{3}\,\frac{{\cal F}^{j}_{\left(5\right)}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{4}}+\pi\left(\frac{{\cal F}^{j}_{\left(2\right)}}{d_{\sigma}}+\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{{\cal F}^{j}_{\left(4\right)}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}+\frac{3}{8}\,\frac{{\cal F}^{j}_{\left(6\right)}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{5}}-\frac{1}{6}\,\frac{{\cal G}^{j}_{\left(5\right)}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}\right).

(312)

By inserting the coefficients
{\cal F}^{j}_{\left(n\right)}
and
{\cal G}^{j}_{\left(5\right)}
into (
312
) one obtains

\displaystyle\left[\sum\limits_{n=2}^{6}{\cal F}^{j}_{\left(n\right)}\dot{\cal X}^{\infty}_{\left(n\right)}+{\cal G}^{j}_{\left(5\right)}\dot{\cal Y}^{\infty}_{\left(5\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\,\frac{x_{0}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{4}}d_{\sigma}^{j}-\frac{15}{4}\,\pi\,\frac{d_{\sigma}^{j}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}

(313)

where all terms proportional to
\sigma^{j}
have been omitted since they do not contribute to the total
light deflection because of
\epsilon^{ijk}\sigma^{i}\sigma^{j}=0
. Inserting (

)
into (

) yields for the absolute value

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\,\left|\frac{15}{4}\,\frac{\pi}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}-8\,\frac{x_{0}+\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{3}}\right|\,,

(314)

which is the asserted relation (
108
) and agrees with the 2PN terms in Eq. (3.2.44) in
Brumberg1991

as well as with the 2PN terms in Eq. (65) in

, if one uses the PPN parameters in the limit of general relativity.
The upper limit (
314
) is valid for any astrometric configuration of source, body, and observer, that means
for
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\leq 0
as well as for
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}\geq 0
. We are mainly interested in
astrometric configurations (cf. condition (
111
))

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$x$}_{0}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 0\;,

(315)

that means astrometric configurations where the massive body is located between the light source and the observer.
In general, condition (
315
) holds for any light source far from the Solar System, like stellar light sources.
If condition (
315
) is assumed, then the upper limit (

) simplifies as follows:

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\times\mbox{\boldmath$\nu$}^{{\rm M}\times{\rm M}}_{\rm 2PN}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{15}{4}\,\pi\,\frac{G^{2}M^{2}}{c^{4}}\,\frac{1}{\left(d_{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\;,

(316)

which is the asserted relation (
112
